His white head moved from side to side beneath it rhythmically, or lunged and recovered with the fierceness of a duelist thrusting, but he was magnificently the master of his giant, and it sang to his magic as he bade it.

Bibbs was swept away upon that mighty singing. Such a thing was wholly unknown to him; there had been no music in his meager life. Unlike the tale, it was the Princess Bedrulbudour who had brought him to the enchanted cave, and that﻿—for Bibbs﻿—was what made its magic dazing. It seemed to him a long, long time since he had been walking home drearily from Dr. Gurney’s office; it seemed to him that he had set out upon a happy journey since then, and that he had reached another planet, where Mary Vertrees and he sat alone together listening to a vast choiring of invisible soldiers and holy angels. There were armies of voices about them singing praise and thanksgiving; and yet they were alone. It was incredible that the walls of the church were not the boundaries of the universe, to remain so forever; incredible that there was a smoky street just yonder, where housemaids were bringing in evening papers from front steps and where children were taking their last spins on roller-skates before being haled indoors for dinner.

===Chinese===
“不，”玛丽望着他，淡淡一笑，“您不会的。”

一阵宏大的乐声开始在空中浮游、震颤，她随即转开了脸；教堂里那片巨大空阔的空间盛满了它，听着的那两个人也被它盛满；整个宇宙仿佛都随它充盈而震颤。两个人凝然端坐，那宏大的声音四下环绕，教堂渐渐昏暗下去，只剩风琴师的一盏灯结成一点星子似的光。他那颗白头在灯下有节奏地左右摆动，忽而如剑客挺刺一般猛然前扑、又稳稳收住；然而他威风堂堂，是他那头巨怪的主人，巨怪应着他的魔法歌唱，他吩咐唱什么，它便唱什么。

比布斯被那浩大的歌唱卷走了。这样的事于他全然陌生；他那份寒伧的生涯里从来没有过音乐。不像故事里讲的那样：领他走进这座魔窟的，是白狄伦·布杜鲁（Bedrulbudour）公主；而在比布斯，这正是那魔法叫人目眩神迷的缘由。在他感觉里，他灰溜溜地从格尼医生的诊所走回家去，已是许久许久以前的事了；他觉得自己自那以后踏上了一段快乐的旅程，来到了另一颗行星，在这颗星上，玛丽·弗特里斯同他并肩独坐，听一支由看不见的士兵和圣洁天使组成的大合唱。四下里人声的军团唱着赞美与感恩；可他们两个仍是独自在一起。教堂的四壁竟不是宇宙的边界，而且竟不会永远如此，这真叫人难以置信；同样难以置信的是，就在那边不远的地方，竟还有一条烟雾弥漫的街，街上的女仆正把晚报从前门台阶上收进屋去，孩子们正趁着被拖进屋吃晚饭之前，蹬着轮滑鞋溜最后几圈。

===Original===
He had a curious sense of communication with his new friend. He knew it could not be so, and yet he felt as if all the time he spoke to her, saying: “You hear this strain? You hear that strain? You know the dream that these sounds bring to me?” And it seemed to him as though she answered continually: “I hear! I hear that strain, and I hear the new one that you are hearing now. I know the dream that these sounds bring to you. Yes, yes, I hear it all! We hear﻿—together!”

And though the church grew so dim that all was mysterious shadow except the vague planes of the windows and the organist’s light, with the white head moving beneath it, Bibbs had no consciousness that the girl sitting beside him had grown shadowy; he seemed to see her as plainly as ever in the darkness, though he did not look at her. And all the mighty chanting of the organ’s multitudinous voices that afternoon seemed to Bibbs to be chorusing of her and interpreting her, singing her thoughts and singing for him the world of humble gratitude that was in his heart because she was so kind to him. It all meant Mary.

===Chinese===
他对自己这位新朋友怀着一种奇异的声气相通之感。他知道事情并非如此，然而他却觉得自己仿佛一直在同她说话：“这一段曲调您听见了吗？那一段曲调您听见了吗？这些乐音带给我的梦，您知道吗？”而他觉得她仿佛一直在应答：“我听见了！我听见了那一段曲调，也听见了你这会儿正听着的新的一段。这些乐音带给你的梦，我知道。是的，是的，我全都听见了！我们在——一起听！”

教堂越来越暗，暗得除了窗子隐约的轮廓和风琴师的那点灯光——灯下那颗白头还在移动——一切都成了神秘的暗影；可比布斯丝毫没有察觉，身旁坐着的姑娘也已隐入昏暗；黑暗里她在他眼中依然清晰如初，尽管他并没有看她。那天下午，管风琴万千声部全部浩大的咏唱，在比布斯听来都像是在齐声咏唱她、诠释她，唱出她的心思，也替他唱出他心里那谦卑感激的一片世界——因为她待他这样好。这一切说的都是玛丽。

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
But when she asked him what it meant, on their homeward way, he was silent. They had come a few paces from the church without speaking, walking slowly.

“I’ll tell you what it meant to me,” she said, as he did not immediately reply. “Almost any music of Handel’s always means one thing above all others to me: courage! That’s it. It makes cowardice of whining seem so infinitesimal﻿—it makes *most* things in our hustling little lives seem infinitesimal.”

“Yes,” he said. “It seems odd, doesn’t it, that people downtown are hurrying to trains and hanging to straps in trolley-cars, weltering every way to get home and feed and sleep so they can get downtown tomorrow. And yet there isn’t anything down there worth getting to. They’re like servants drudging to keep the house going, and believing the drudgery itself is the great thing. They make so much noise and fuss and dirt they forget that the house was meant to live in. The housework has to be done, but the people who do it have been so overpaid that they’re confused and worship the housework. They’re overpaid, and yet, poor things! they haven’t anything that a chicken can’t have. Of course, when the world gets to paying its wages sensibly that will be different.”

===Chinese===
可是在归途上，当她问他那是什么意思时，他沉默了。两人已经从教堂走出几步远，一路无话，缓缓地走着。

“我来告诉你它对我是什么意思，”她说，因为他没有立刻作声。“几乎凡是亨德尔（Handel）的乐曲，对我总有一层意思高过其余一切：勇气！就是这么回事。它使哭哭啼啼的怯懦显得那么微不足道——它使我们这忙忙乱乱的小小人生里的*大多数*事情，都显得微不足道。”

“是啊，”他说。“说来也怪，是不是？市中心的人赶着去搭火车，在电车车里吊着带子，千般翻腾地往家赶，好吃饱睡足，好明天再到市中心去。可那边其实并没有什么值得赶去的东西。他们像一伙仆役，操劳苦役好叫这座宅子维持下去，还相信苦役本身就是头等大事。他们闹出的响动、忙乱和肮脏太多，倒忘了这宅子本来是住人的。家务总得有人做，可做家务的人得的报酬太过了，过到晕头转向，竟崇拜起家务来。报酬是过多了，可是，可怜的人哪！他们有的一切，一只鸡也照样能有。当然喽，等这世上发工钱发得合乎情理了，情形就不一样了。”

===Original===
“Do you mean ‘communism’?” she asked, and she made their slow pace a little slower﻿—they had only three blocks to go.

“Whatever the word is, I only mean that things don’t look very sensible now﻿—especially to a man that wants to keep out of ’em and can’t! ‘Communism’? Well, at least any decent sport would say it’s fair for all the strong runners to start from the same mark and give the weak ones a fair distance ahead, so that all can run something like even on the stretch. And wouldn’t it be pleasant, really, if they could all cross the winning-line together? Who really enjoys beating anybody﻿—if he sees the beaten man’s face? The only way we can enjoy getting ahead of other people nowadays is by forgetting what the other people feel. And that,” he added, “is nothing of what the music meant to me. You see, if I keep talking about what it didn’t mean I can keep from telling you what it did mean.”

“Didn’t it mean courage to you, too﻿—a little?” she asked. “Triumph and praise were in it, and somehow those things mean courage to me.”

===Chinese===
“你是说‘共产主义’吗？”她问，还把他们迟缓的脚步又放慢了些——他们只剩三个街区了。

“管它那个词儿是什么，我只是说，如今这些事看着不怎么合情理——尤其在一个人眼里，他想躲开这些事，又躲不开！‘共产主义’？嗯，至少任何一个正派的运动家都会说：让所有身强力壮的跑手从同一条起跑线出发，让体弱的先占下一段公道的路程，好叫大家在最后一段冲刺时跑得大致相当，这才算公道。再说，倘若大家真能一齐越过终点线，那岂不真叫人痛快？谁当真能从胜过别人里得到乐趣——如果他看见那张被胜过的人的脸？如今我们要想从压过别人上面得到乐趣，唯一的法子就是忘掉别人心里是什么滋味。而这个，”他补了一句，“全不是那音乐对我的意思。你瞧，我只要一直讲它不是什么意思，就可以一直不告诉你它是什么意思。”

“难道它对你就没有一点勇气的意思吗——一点点？”她问。“里面有凯旋，有颂赞，不知怎么，这几样东西在我听来就是勇气。”

===Original===
“Yes, they were all there,” Bibbs said. “I don’t know the name of what he played, but I shouldn’t think it would matter much. The man that makes the music must leave it to you what it can mean to you, and the name he puts to it can’t make much difference﻿—except to himself and people very much like him, I suppose.”

“I suppose that’s true, though I’d never thought of it like that.”

“I imagine music must make feelings and paint pictures in the minds of the people who hear it,” Bibbs went on, musingly, “according to their own natures as much as according to the music itself. The musician might compose something and play it, wanting you to think of the Holy Grail, and some people who heard it would think of a prayer-meeting, and some would think of how good they were themselves, and a boy might think of himself at the head of a solemn procession, carrying a banner and riding a white horse. And then, if there were some jubilant passages in the music, he’d think of a circus.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，那些都在里面，”比布斯说。“他弹的是什么曲子，我不知道名字，可我想那没有多大要紧。作音乐的人，只能由着它对你是什么意思；他给曲子起的那个名字，变不出多大花样来——我想，除了对他自己，以及和他极相像的人以外。”

“我想这话不错，虽然我从来没有那样想过。”

“我猜音乐在听的人心里唤起情感、画出图画，”比布斯若有所思地接着说，“多半是依着各人自己的天性，同依着音乐本身一样多。作曲的人也许写了一支曲子弹出来，想叫你想起圣杯（Holy Grail），可听的人里头，有人会想起一次祈祷会，有人会想起自己有多么好，有个男孩也许会想见自己走在一支庄严行列的最前头，扛着大旗，骑着白马。然后，倘若曲子里有几段欢腾的乐句，他又会想起马戏场来。”

===Original===
They had reached her gate, and she set her hand upon it, but did not open it. Bibbs felt that this was almost the kindest of her kindnesses﻿—not to be prompt in leaving him.

“After all,” she said, “you didn’t tell me whether you liked it.”

“No. I didn’t need to.”

“No, that’s true, and I didn’t need to ask. I knew. But you said you were trying to keep from telling me what it did mean.”

“I can’t keep from telling it any longer,” he said. “The music meant to me﻿—it meant the kindness of﻿—of you.”

===Chinese===
他们走到了她家门口，她把手搭在院门上，却没有推开。比布斯觉得，这几乎是她种种厚道里最厚道的一桩——她并不急着撇下他走开。

“说到底，”她说，“你还没告诉我你喜欢不喜欢呢。”

“是啊。我用不着说。”

“是啊，这话不错，我也用不着问。我知道。可是你说过，你想瞒着不告诉我它究竟是什么意思。”

“我再也瞒不住了，”他说。“那音乐对我意味着——它意味着——意味着你的厚道。”

===Original===
“Kindness? How?”

“You thought I was a sort of lonely tramp﻿—and sick﻿—”

“No,” she said, decidedly. “I thought perhaps you’d like to hear Dr. Kraft play. And you did.”

“It’s curious; sometimes it seemed to me that it was you who were playing.”

Mary laughed. “I? I strum! Piano. A little Chopin﻿—Grieg﻿—Chaminade. You wouldn’t listen!”

Bibbs drew a deep breath. “I’m frightened again,” he said, in an unsteady voice. “I’m afraid you’ll think I’m pushing, but﻿—” He paused, and the words sank to a murmur.

===Chinese===
“厚道？怎么说？”

“你当我是个孤零零的流浪汉——还是个病人——”

“不是，”她斩钉截铁地说。“我只是想，你也许愿意听听克拉夫特医生（Dr. Kraft）弹琴。你也确实愿意来着。”

“说来奇怪；有时候我觉得，弹琴的倒是你。”

玛丽笑了。“我？我那叫乱弹！钢琴。一点点肖邦（Chopin）——格里格（Grieg）——夏米纳德（Chaminade）。你才不肯听呢！”

比布斯深深吸了一口气。“我又害怕了，”他说，声音发颤。“我怕你会觉得我冒昧，可是——”他顿住了，话音低成一串喃喃。

===Original===
“Oh, if you want *me* to play for you!” she said. “Yes, gladly. It will be merely absurd after what you heard this afternoon. I play like a hundred thousand other girls, and I like it. I’m glad when anyone’s willing to listen, and if you﻿—” She stopped, checked by a sudden recollection, and laughed ruefully. “But my piano won’t be here after tonight. I﻿—I’m sending it away tomorrow. I’m afraid that if you’d like me to play to you you’d have to come this evening.”

“You’ll let me?” he cried.

“Certainly, if you care to.”

“If *I* could play﻿—” he said, wistfully, “if I could play like that old man in the church I could thank you.”

“Ah, but you haven’t heard me play. I *know* you liked this afternoon, but﻿—”

“Yes,” said Bibbs. “It was the greatest happiness I’ve ever known.”

===Chinese===
“哦，你要的是*我*弹给你听呀！”她说。“愿意，很乐意。听过你今天下午听的那一席，我这弹奏简直是笑话了。我弹得同十万别的姑娘一个样，可我喜欢弹。有人肯听我就高兴，要是你——”她忽然记起一件事，顿住了，苦笑起来。“可是今晚一过，我的钢琴就不在这儿了。我——我明天要把它送走。我怕的是，你若想叫我弹给你听，就得今天晚上来。”

“你肯让我来？”他叫道。

“当然，只要你愿意。”

“要是我会弹——”他说，怅怅地，“要是我能像教堂里那位老人家那样弹，我就能够谢谢你了。”

“啊，可你还没听过我弹呢。我*知道*你喜欢今天下午，可是——”

“是啊，”比布斯说。“那是我这辈子所知道的最大幸福。”

===Original===
It was too dark to see his face, but his voice held such plain honesty, and he spoke with such complete unconsciousness of saying anything especially significant, that she knew it was the truth. For a moment she was nonplussed, then she opened the gate and went in. “You’ll come after dinner, then?”

“Yes,” he said, not moving. “Would you mind if I stood here until time to come in?”

She had reached the steps, and at that she turned, offering him the response of laughter and a gay gesture of her muff toward the lighted windows of the New House, as though bidding him to run home to his dinner.

That night, Bibbs sat writing in his notebook.

===Chinese===
天太黑，看不见他的脸，可他的嗓音里有一种明明白白的诚实，他说话又是那样浑然不觉自己说出了什么分外要紧的话，因此她知道这是真话。她一时间没了主意，随后推开院门走了进去。“那么，你吃过晚饭来？”

“好，”他说，人没有动。“要是我就在这儿站着，站到该进去的时辰，你介意吗？”

她已经走到台阶前，听了这话便回转身来，笑着答他，又把手筒朝新宅那几扇亮着灯的窗户俏皮地一扬，仿佛催他跑回家去吃晚饭。

那天夜里，比布斯坐着在他的笔记本上写字。

===Original===
> Music can come into a blank life, and fill it. Everything that is beautiful is music, if you can listen.

> There is no gracefulness like that of a graceful woman at a grand piano. There is a swimming loveliness of line that seems to merge with the running of the sound, and you seem, as you watch her, to see what you are hearing and to hear what you are seeing.

> There are women who make you think of pine woods coming down to a sparkling sea. The air about such a woman is bracing, and when she is near you, you feel strong and ambitious; you forget that the world doesn’t like you. You think that perhaps you are a great fellow, after all. Then you come away and feel like a boy who has fallen in love with his Sunday-school teacher. You’ll be whipped for it﻿—and ought to be.

> There are women who make you think of Diana, crowned with the moon. But they do not have the Greek profile. I do not believe Helen of Troy had a Greek profile; they would not have fought about her if her nose had been quite that long. The Greek nose is not the adorable nose. The adorable nose is about an eighth of an inch shorter.

===Chinese===
> 音乐能走进一个空白的人生，把它填满。凡是美的东西都是音乐，只要你听得见。

> 再没有什么风致比得上一个在三角钢琴前姿态优雅的女人。有一种流动的线条之美，仿佛与流转的乐音融成一体；你望着她，仿佛看得见自己听见的东西，听得见自己看见的东西。

> 有一种女人叫你想起松林直下到波光闪动的大海。这样的女人周身的空气都提神，她在你近旁的时候，你觉得自己强壮，雄心勃勃；你忘了这世界并不喜欢你。你想，说到底，你也许还算个了不起的人物。随后你走了，又觉得自己像个爱上了主日学校女教师的男孩。你会为此挨鞭子——而且挨得不冤。

> 有一种女人叫你想起戴安娜（Diana），头上戴着月亮的冠冕。可她们并没有那种希腊式的侧影。我不信特洛伊的海伦（Helen of Troy）长着希腊式的侧影；倘若她的鼻子当真有那么长，人们才不会为她开战呢。希腊鼻子不是可爱的鼻子。可爱的鼻子大约要短八分之一英寸。

===Original===
> Much of the music of Wagner, it appears, is not suitable to the piano. Wagner was a composer who could interpret into music such things as the primitive impulses of humanity﻿—he could have made a machine-shop into music. But not if he had to work in it. Wagner was always dealing in immensities﻿—a machine-shop would have put a majestic lump in so grand a gizzard as that.

> There is a mystery about pianos, it seems. Sometimes they have to be “sent away.” That is how some people speak of the penitentiary. “Sent away” is a euphuism for “sent to prison.” But pianos are not sent to prison, and they are not sent to the tuner﻿—the tuner is sent to them. Why are pianos “sent away”﻿—and where?

> Sometimes a glorious day shines into the most ordinary and useless life. Happiness and beauty come caroling out of the air into the gloomy house of that life as if some stray angel just happened to perch on the rooftree, resting and singing. And the night after such a day is lustrous and splendid with the memory of it. Music and beauty and kindness﻿—those are the three greatest things God can give us. To bring them all in one day to one who expected nothing﻿—ah! the heart that received them should be as humble as it is thankful. But it is hard to be humble when one is so rich with new memories. It is impossible to be humble after a day of glory.

> Yes﻿—the adorable nose is more than an eighth of an inch shorter than the Greek nose. It is a full quarter of an inch shorter.

> There are women who will be kinder to a sick tramp than to a conquering hero. But the sick tramp had better remember that’s what he is. Take care, take care! Humble’s the word!

===Chinese===
> 看样子，瓦格纳（Wagner）的许多乐曲并不适合钢琴。瓦格纳是这样一位作曲家：他能把人类原始的冲动这类东西译成音乐——他能把一间机器车间谱成音乐。可前提是他不必自己在里头干活。瓦格纳经手的向来是浩瀚无垠的东西——机器车间那种东西，会叫那么堂皇的一副砂囊里结出一颗庄严的疙瘩。

> 钢琴仿佛也有一种神秘的讲究。有时候，它们不得不“给送走”。有些人说到送进监牢，用的正是这个说法。“给送走”是“送进监狱”的文雅说法。可钢琴并不是给送进监狱，也不是给送去调音师那儿——是调音师给送到它们那儿去。钢琴为什么“给送走”——又送到哪儿去呢？

> 有时候，一个光辉的日子会照进最平庸、最无用的人生。幸福和美从半空里唱着歌儿来，降临到这人生的暗淡屋子里，仿佛一只迷路的天使恰好落在屋脊上，歇着脚，唱着歌。这样的日子过后，那一晚便因这段记忆而光华灿烂。音乐、美和厚道——这是上帝所能赐给我们的三样最大的东西。要在一天之内把它们一齐带给一个本来什么都不指望的人——啊！那颗受了它们的心，应当同它感激一样地谦卑。可一个人新记忆这样富足的时候，谦卑是很难的。经过了一天荣光，要谦卑是不可能的。

> 是啊——可爱的鼻子比希腊鼻子不止短八分之一英寸。它足足短了四分之一英寸。

> 有一种女人，待一个病中的流浪汉会比待一位凯旋的英雄更厚道。可是那病中的流浪汉最好记住自己是什么人。当心，当心！“谦卑”二字才是正经！

## XVII / 十七

===Original===

That “mystery about pianos” which troubled Bibbs had been a mystery to Mr. Vertrees, and it was being explained to him at about the time Bibbs scribbled the reference to it in his notes. Mary had gone upstairs upon Bibbs’s departure at ten o’clock, and Mr. and Mrs. Vertrees sat until after midnight in the library, talking. And in all that time they found not one cheerful topic, but became more depressed with everything and with every phase of everything that they discussed﻿—no extraordinary state of affairs in a family which has always “held up its head,” only to arrive in the end at a point where all it can do is to look on helplessly at the processes of its own financial dissolution. For that was the point which this despairing couple had reached﻿—they could do nothing except look on and talk about it. They were only vaporing, and they knew it.

“She needn’t to have done that about her piano,” vapored Mr. Vertrees. “We could have managed somehow without it. At least she ought to have consulted me, and if she insisted I could have arranged the details with the﻿—the dealer.”

“She thought that it might be﻿—annoying for you,” Mrs. Vertrees explained. “Really, she planned for you not to know about it until they had removed﻿—until after tomorrow, that is, but I decided to﻿—to mention it. You see, she didn’t even tell me about it until this morning. She has another idea, too, I’m afraid. It’s﻿—it’s﻿—”

===Chinese===

那桩困扰着比布斯的“钢琴之谜”，在弗特里斯先生这里一向也是桩谜，而谜底正对着他讲解开来，恰在比布斯于笔记里草草记上一笔的前后。十点钟比布斯一走，玛丽便上了楼，弗特里斯先生和太太在书房里一直坐到半夜过后，谈着话。这整整一段时间里，他们找不出一件快活的题目，谈什么，什么便连同它的每一层枝节都叫他们愈发颓丧——这对一个向来“抬着头”撑门面的家，并不是什么稀罕境况：撑到最后，只落到这么一步，除了束手旁观自家财政分崩离析的过程，再无别的事可做。这对绝望的夫妻正是到了这一步——除了看着，谈着，他们什么也做不了。他们不过是在放空炮，而且自己也知道。

“她其实犯不着这样处置她的钢琴，”弗特里斯先生唉声叹气地说。“少了那笔钱，我们总还能想出个法子对付。至少她该先跟我商量一声；她既然执意要办，细节我本可以亲自去跟那——那家琴行交涉。”

“她是怕那会让你——难堪，”弗特里斯太太解释说。“真的，她原打算叫你蒙在鼓里，等他们把琴搬走——就是说，等到后天，可我决定还是——还是提一句。你想，她连我也是到今天早上才告诉的。恐怕她还有一个主意。那是——那是——”

===Original===

“Well?” he urged, as she found it difficult to go on.

“Her other idea is﻿—that is, it was﻿—I think it can be avoided, of course﻿—it was about her furs.”

“No!” he exclaimed, quickly. “I won’t have it! You must see to that. I’d rather not talk to her about it, but you mustn’t let her.”

===Chinese===

“嗯？”他催促道；她一时接不下去。

“她的另一个主意是——就是说，是曾经打算——当然，我想那是可以打住的——是关于她的皮衣。”

“不行！”他立刻嚷道。“我不同意！这件事你得给我拦住。我不愿意自己去跟她谈，可你千万不能让她那么办。”

===Original===

“I’ll try not,” his wife promised. “Of course, they’re very handsome.”

“All the more reason for her to keep them!” he returned, irritably. “We’re not *that* far gone, I think!”

“Perhaps not yet,” Mrs. Vertrees said. “She seems to be troubled about the﻿—the coal matter and﻿—about Tilly. Of course the piano will take care of some things like those for a while and﻿—”

“I don’t like it. I gave her the piano to play on, not to﻿—”

===Chinese===

“我尽量拦着，”他妻子应承道。“不过，那几件皮衣确实很漂亮。”

“那她就更该留着！”他不耐烦地回嘴。“我想咱们还没落到那个地步！”

“眼下也许还没有，”弗特里斯太太说。“她好像在愁那——那笔煤钱，还有——还有蒂莉（Tilly）。当然了，钢琴能把这些事应付一阵子，而且——”

“我不喜欢这样。我把钢琴给她，是给她弹的，不是叫她——”

===Original===

“You mustn’t be distressed about it in *one* way,” she said, comfortingly. “She arranged with the﻿—with the purchaser that the men will come for it about half after five in the afternoon. The days are so short now it’s really quite winter.”

“Oh, yes,” he agreed, moodily. “So far as that goes people have a right to move a piece of furniture without stirring up the neighbors, I suppose, even by daylight. I don’t suppose *our* neighbors are paying much attention just now, though I hear Sheridan was back in his office early the morning after the funeral.”

Mrs. Vertrees made a little sound of commiseration. “I don’t believe that was because he wasn’t suffering, though. I’m sure it was only because he felt his business was so important. Mary told me he seemed wrapped up in his son’s succeeding; and that was what he bragged about most. He isn’t vulgar in his boasting, I understand; he doesn’t talk a great deal about his﻿—his actual money﻿—though there was something about blades of grass that I didn’t comprehend. I think he meant something about his energy﻿—but perhaps not. No, his bragging usually seemed to be not so much a personal vainglory as about his family and the greatness of this city.”

===Chinese===

“只有一层你不必难过，”她安慰道。“她跟那——那位买主讲好了，下午五点半左右来人搬琴。如今天短得很，简直已经入冬了。”

“哦，是啊，”他闷闷地附和。“就这一层说，人家搬一件家具不想惊动邻居，我想也在情理之中，哪怕在大白天搬。我想咱们自家的邻居眼下也没多少心思放在咱们身上，不过我听说，葬礼第二天一早谢里丹就回办公室去了。”

弗特里斯太太低低地同情了一声。“不过我相信，那并不是他心里不苦。我敢说那只是因为他觉得自己的买卖太要紧。玛丽告诉我，他一门心思全扑在儿子接他的班上；这也是他夸口夸得最凶的。据我看，他夸起来倒不俗气；他不怎么谈他那些——实实在在的钱——虽然有一段关于草叶的话我没听明白。我想他那意思是在夸自己的精力——可也许不是。不，他的吹嘘通常不像是个人的虚荣，倒更像夸他的家族，夸这座城的伟大。”

===Original===

“ ‘Greatness of this city’!” Mr. Vertrees echoed, with dull bitterness. “It’s nothing but a coal-hole! I suppose it looks ‘great’ to the man who has the luck to make it work for him. I suppose it looks ‘great’ to any *young* man, too, starting out to make his fortune out of it. The fellows that get what they want out of it say it’s ‘great,’ and everybody else gets the habit. But you have a different point of view if it’s the city that got what it wanted out of you! Of course Sheridan says it’s ‘great.’ ”

Mrs. Vertrees seemed unaware of this unusual outburst. “I believe,” she began, timidly, “he doesn’t boast of﻿—that is, I understand he has never seemed so interested in the﻿—the other one.”

Her husband’s face was dark, but at that a heavier shadow fell upon it; he looked more haggard than before. “ ‘The other one,’ ” he repeated, averting his eyes. “You mean﻿—you mean the third son﻿—the one that was here this evening?”

===Chinese===

“‘这座城的伟大’！”弗特里斯先生跟着念了一遍，苦得发木。“它不过是个煤窟！对一个有运气能驱使它替自己卖命的人，它看上去自然‘伟大’。对随便哪个年轻轻的小伙子，初出茅庐要从它身上发财的，它看上去自然也‘伟大’。从它身上捞到好处的家伙们说它‘伟大’，其余的人便都跟着染上了这口头禅。可要是轮到它从你身上捞走了它想要的东西，你的看法就会两样！谢里丹自然要说它‘伟大’。”

弗特里斯太太似乎没察觉丈夫这番少见的发作。“我想，”她怯怯地开口，“他不夸那个——就是说，据我了解，他好像从来不曾把心思放在另一个身上。”

她丈夫的脸色本是阴沉的，听了这话，上面又罩下一层更重的暗影；他看上去比先前更憔悴了。“‘另一个’，”他重复道，避开了她的眼睛。“你是说——你是说第三个儿子——今晚来的那一个？”

===Original===

“Yes, the﻿—the youngest,” she returned, her voice so feeble it was almost a whisper.

And then neither of them spoke for several long minutes. Nor did either look at the other during that silence.

At last Mr. Vertrees contrived to cough, but not convincingly. “What﻿—ah﻿—what was it Mary said about him out in the hall, when she came in this afternoon? I heard you asking her something about him, but she answered in such a low voice I didn’t﻿—ah﻿—happen to catch it.”

===Chinese===

“是的，最——最小的那一个，”她应道，声音微弱得几乎成了耳语。

之后好几分钟，两个人谁也没有作声。在那片沉默里，谁也没有看谁一眼。

末了弗特里斯先生勉强咳了一声，可咳得不像。“玛丽今天下午进门的时候，在外头门厅里说他什么来着？我听见你问过他几句，可她答话的声音那么低，我没——呃——没凑巧听清。”

===Original===

“She﻿—she didn’t say much. All she said was this: I asked her if she had enjoyed her walk with him, and she said, ‘He’s the most wistful creature I’ve ever known.’ ”

“Well?”

“That was all. He *is* wistful-looking; and so fragile﻿—though he doesn’t seem quite so much so lately. I was watching Mary from the window when she went out today, and he joined her, and if I hadn’t known about him I’d have thought he had quite an interesting face.”

===Chinese===

“她——她没说多少。她说的就这一句：我问她跟他散步散得可好，她说：‘他是我平生见过的最惹人怜惜的人。’”

“后来呢？”

“就这一句。他那模样确实惹人怜惜，人也单薄——虽然近来似乎没那么单薄了。今天玛丽出门的时候，我在窗口望着她，他赶上她，跟她做伴；我若是不知道他的底细，会当他那张脸还挺耐看呢。”

===Original===

“If you ‘hadn’t known about him’? Known what?”

“Oh, nothing, of course,” she said, hurriedly. “Nothing definite, that is. Mary said decidely, long ago, that he’s not at all insane, as we thought at first. It’s only﻿—well, of course it *is* odd, their attitude about him. I suppose it’s some nervous trouble that makes him﻿—perhaps a little queer at times, so that he can’t apply himself to anything﻿—or perhaps does odd things. But, after all, of course, we only have an impression about it. We don’t know﻿—that is, positively. I﻿—” She paused, then went on: “I didn’t know just how to ask﻿—that is﻿—I didn’t mention it to Mary. I didn’t﻿—I﻿—” The poor lady floundered pitifully, concluding with a mumble. “So soon after﻿—after the﻿—the shock.”

“I don’t think I’ve caught more than a glimpse of him,” said Mr. Vertrees. “I wouldn’t know him if I saw him, but your impression of him is﻿—” He broke off suddenly, springing to his feet in agitation. “I can’t imagine her﻿—oh, *no*!” he gasped. And he began to pace the floor. “A half-witted epileptic!”

===Chinese===

“你若是‘不知道他的底细’？不知道什么？”

“哦，没什么，当然，”她慌忙说。“没什么确切的事，我是说。玛丽老早就一口咬定，他全然不像我们起初以为的那样疯。只不过——唉，他们一家人对他那副态度，当然着实蹊跷。我想他是有某种神经上的毛病，弄得他——也许有时候有点怪，什么事都安不下心去做——或者会干出些古怪事。可是说到底，咱们也不过有点印象罢了。咱们并不知——就是说不确切。我——”她顿了顿，又往下说：“我不知道该怎么打听才好——就是说——我没跟玛丽提。我没有——我——”可怜的太太狼狈不堪，末了含混了一句收场。“事情刚过去——刚出了那——那桩变故。”

“我想我顶多远远看过他一眼，”弗特里斯先生说。“见了面也未必认得出；不过照你的印象，他是——”他猛然收住，激动得从椅子里跳起来。“我不能想象她——哦，不行！”他倒抽一口气，开始在地板上来回地踱。“一个半痴的癫痫病人！”

===Original===

“No, no!” she cried. “He may be all right. We﻿—”

“Oh, it’s horrible! I can’t﻿—” He threw himself back into his chair again, sweeping his hands across his face, then letting them fall limply at his sides.

Mrs. Vertrees was tremulous. “You mustn’t give way so,” she said, inspired for once almost to direct discourse. “Whatever Mary might think of doing, it wouldn’t be on her own account; it would be on ours. But if *we* should﻿—should consider it, that wouldn’t be on *our* own account. It isn’t because we think of ourselves.”

===Chinese===

“不，不！”她喊道。“他兴许好好的。咱们——”

“哦，太可怕了！我不敢——”他整个人跌回椅子里，两手在脸上一抹，随即软软地垂在身侧。

弗特里斯太太浑身发颤。“你不该这样垮掉，”她说，一辈子难得有一回，几乎被激得把话挑得直截了当。“玛丽不管打算怎么做，都不是为她自己，是为了咱们。可咱们若是——若是考虑这件事，也不是为咱们自己。咱们不是替自己打算。”

===Original===

“Oh God, no!” he groaned. “Not for us! We can go to the poorhouse, but Mary can’t be a stenographer!”

Sighing, Mrs. Vertrees resumed her obliqueness. “Of course,” she murmured, “it all seems very premature, speculating about such things, but I had a queer sort of feeling that she seemed quite interested in this﻿—” She had almost said “in this one,” but checked herself. “In this young man. It’s natural, of course; she is always so strong and well, and he is﻿—he seems to be, that is﻿—rather appealing to the﻿—the sympathies.”

“Yes!” he agreed, bitterly. “Precisely. The sympathies!”

===Chinese===

“哦，天哪，不是！”他呻吟道。“不是为咱们！咱们进济贫院都行，可玛丽不能去当速记员！”

弗特里斯太太叹了口气，又绕起了弯子。“当然了，”她低声说，“这样揣度这些事，全显得为时太早，不过我有种说不出的感觉，她好像对这一位挺上心——”她差点说出“对这一个”，赶紧收住。“对这位年轻人。这自然不奇怪；她向来健壮康泰，而他呢——他好像，我是说——挺惹人起——起同情的。”

“是啊！”他惨然应道。“一点不差。同情！”

===Original===

“Perhaps,” she faltered, “perhaps you might feel easier if I could have a little talk with someone?”

“With whom?”

“I had thought of﻿—not going about it too brusquely, of course, but perhaps just waiting for his name to be mentioned, if I happened to be talking with somebody that knew the family﻿—and then I might find a chance to say that I was sorry to hear he’d been ill so much, and﻿—Something of that kind perhaps?”

===Chinese===

“也许，”她嗫嚅道，“倘若我能找什么人稍微谈谈，你心里兴许松快些？”

“跟谁？”

“我是想——当然不会莽莽撞撞地开口，也许只消等他的名字被提起，倘若我恰好在跟什么认识他们家的人闲谈——那时候我或许能寻个机会，说听说他病了那么久，心里着实难过，再——诸如此类的话，也许？”

===Original===

“You don’t know anybody that knows the family.”

“Yes. That is﻿—well, in a way, of course, one *of* the family. That Mrs. Roscoe Sheridan is not a﻿—that is, she’s rather a pleasant-faced little woman, I think, and of course rather ordinary. I think she is interested about﻿—that is, of course, she’d be anxious to be more intimate with Mary, naturally. She’s always looking over here from her house; she was looking out the window this afternoon when Mary went out, I noticed﻿—though I don’t think Mary saw her. I’m sure she wouldn’t think it out of place to﻿—to be frank about matters. She called the other day, and Mary must rather like her﻿—she said that evening that the call had done her good. Don’t you think it might be wise?”

“Wise? I don’t know. I feel the whole matter is impossible.”

===Chinese===

“你并不认识什么认识他们家的人。”

“有啊。就是——唉，说起来，其中一位当然算是他们家里的人。那位罗斯科·谢里丹太太并不是个——就是说，我觉得她那张小脸倒满和气，当然人也平常。我看她有心——就是说，她自然巴望着跟玛丽亲近些，这是人之常情。她老是从她家那边往这边张望；今天下午玛丽出门的时候她正望着窗外，我留意到了——不过玛丽没瞧见她，我想。她这个人，我相信不至于觉得把事情说——说得直率些有什么失体。前几天她来过，玛丽想必挺喜欢她——那天晚上她说，这一趟来访叫她心里痛快了些。你觉得这么办明智不明智？”

“明智？我不知道。我只觉得整件事都无从谈起。”

===Original===

“Yes, so do I,” she returned, promptly. “It isn’t really a thing we should be considering seriously, of course. Still﻿—”

“I should say not! But possibly﻿—”

Thus they skirmished up and down the field, but before they turned the lights out and went upstairs it was thoroughly understood between them that Mrs. Vertrees should seek the earliest opportunity to obtain definite information from Sibyl Sheridan concerning the mental and physical status of Bibbs. And if he were subject to attacks of lunacy, the unhappy pair decided to prevent the sacrifice they supposed their daughter intended to make of herself. Altogether, if there were spiteful ghosts in the old house that night, eavesdropping upon the woeful comedy, they must have died anew of laughter!

===Chinese===

“是啊，我也这么觉得，”她立刻应道。“这本来也不是咱们该认真考虑的事，当然。不过——”

“当然不该！可是或许——”

就这样，两口子在这片战场上忽进忽退地拉锯；可在熄灯上楼之前，他们之间已经把话说得透透的：弗特里斯太太须抓住最早的时机，向西比尔·谢里丹探明比布斯的神智与身体状况的确信。而倘若他真会疯病发作，这对不幸的夫妇决定阻止他们认为女儿打算做出的自我牺牲。总而言之，那晚老宅里若真有什么促狭恶意的鬼魂，偷听了这出悲惨的喜剧，它们准得笑死个第二回！

===Original===

Mrs. Vertrees’s opportunity occurred the very next afternoon. Darkness had fallen, and the piano-movers had come. They were carrying the piano down the front steps, and Mrs. Vertrees was standing in the open doorway behind them, preparing to withdraw, when she heard a sharp exclamation; and Mrs. Roscoe Sheridan, bareheaded, emerged from the shadow into the light of the doorway.

“Good gracious!” she cried. “It did give me a fright!”

“It’s Mrs. Sheridan, isn’t it?” Mrs. Vertrees was perplexed by this informal appearance, but she reflected that it might be providential. “Won’t you come in?”

“No. Oh no, thank you!” Sibyl panted, pressing her hand to her side. “You don’t know what a fright you’ve given me! And it was nothing but your piano!” She laughed shrilly. “You know, since our tragedy coming so suddenly the other day, you have no idea how upset I’ve been﻿—almost hysterical! And I just glanced out of the window, a minute or so ago, and saw your door wide open and black figures of men against the light, carrying something heavy, and I almost fainted. You see, it was just the way it looked when I saw them bringing my poor brother-in-law in, next door, only such a few short days ago. And I thought I’d seen your daughter start for a drive with Bibbs Sheridan in a car about three o’clock﻿—and﻿—They aren’t back yet, are they?”

“No. Good heavens!”

===Chinese===

弗特里斯太太的机会就在第二天下午来了。天已经黑透，搬钢琴的人也到了。他们正把钢琴抬下门前的台阶，弗特里斯太太站在他们身后敞开的门口，正要退进屋里，忽听得一声短促的惊呼；罗斯科·谢里丹太太没戴帽子，从暗地里走出来，踏进了门里的灯光。

“老天爷！”她喊道。“可真吓了我一跳！”

“您是谢里丹太太吧？”门口这副不拘礼的架势叫弗特里斯太太一时纳闷，可她转念一想，这或许是天意。“进来坐坐好吗？”

“不了。哦，不啦，多谢！”西比尔气喘吁吁，一手按着腰侧。“您不知道您给了我多大的惊吓！原来不过是您家的钢琴！”她尖声笑了。“您瞧，自打前儿个咱们家那桩横祸天外飞来，您不知道我给搅和成什么样子——几乎歇斯底里了！刚才一会儿以前，我朝窗外瞥了一眼，看见您家大门敞着，灯影里几个黑影抬着一件沉东西，我差点晕过去。您想，不多几天前，我瞧见他们把我不幸的小叔子抬进隔壁那会儿，一模一样的光景。我又想起三点来钟的光景，好像看见您女儿跟比布斯·谢里丹坐汽车出门兜风——他们还没回来，是吧？”

“没呢。我的老天爷！”

===Original===

“And the only thing I could think of was that something must have happened to them, and I just dashed over﻿—and it was only your *piano*!” She broke into laughter again. “I suppose you’re just sending it somewhere to be repaired, aren’t you?”

“It’s﻿—it’s being taken downtown,” said Mrs. Vertrees. “Won’t you come in and make me a little visit. I was *so* sorry, the other day, that I was﻿—ah﻿—” She stopped inconsequently, then repeated her invitation. “Won’t you come in? I’d really﻿—”

“Thank you, but I must be running back. My husband usually gets home about this time, and I make a little point of it always to be there.”

===Chinese===

“我满脑子只有一个念头：他们准是出了事，我就一溜烟跑了过来——原来不过是您家的钢琴！”她又咯咯笑起来。“我想您是送去什么地方修吧，是不是？”

“是——是送去城里，”弗特里斯太太说。“进来坐坐，陪我小坐一会儿吧。那天实在叫——呃——叫人过意不去。”她没头没尾地收住话头，又把邀请重复了一遍。“进来坐坐不好吗？我实在是——”

“多谢，可我得赶紧回去了。我丈夫平时这时候到家，我总给自己立个规矩，一定守在家里。”

===Original===

“That’s very sweet.” Mrs. Vertrees descended the steps and walked toward the street with Sibyl. “It’s quite balmy for so late in November, isn’t it? Almost like a May evening.”

“I’m afraid Miss Vertrees will miss her piano,” said Sibyl, watching the instrument disappear into the big van at the curb. “She plays wonderfully, Mrs. Kittersby tells me.”

“Yes, she plays very well. One of your relatives came to hear her yesterday, after dinner, and I think she played all evening for him.”

“You mean Bibbs?” asked Sibyl.

===Chinese===

“真是个贤惠心思。”弗特里斯太太下了台阶，陪着西比尔朝街边走去。“十一月下旬还这么暖和，真难得，是不是？简直像五月里的傍晚。”

“只怕弗特里斯小姐要舍不得她那架钢琴了，”西比尔说，眼睛望着那件乐器消失在路边的大货车里。“基特斯比太太告诉我，她弹得好极了。”

“是啊，她弹得很好。昨天晚饭后，你们家一位亲戚来听过她弹琴，我看她给他弹了一晚上。”

“您是说比布斯？”西比尔问。

===Original===

“The﻿—the youngest Mr. Sheridan. Yes. He’s very musical, isn’t he?”

“I never heard of it. But I shouldn’t think it would matter much whether he was or not, if he could get Miss Vertrees to play to him. Does your daughter expect the piano back soon?”

“I﻿—I believe not immediately. Mr. Sheridan came last evening to hear her play because she had arranged with the﻿—that is, it was to be removed this afternoon. He seems almost well again.”

“Yes.” Sibyl nodded. “His father’s going to try to start him to work.”

===Chinese===

“是——是最小的那位谢里丹先生。是啊。他很有音乐天分，是不是？”

“我从来没听说过。不过我想，只要他能请得动弗特里斯小姐弹给他听，他有没有天分也碍不了什么事。您女儿盼着钢琴很快运回来吗？”

“我——我想不会马上。谢里丹先生昨晚来听她弹琴，因为琴已经说妥要——就是说，定在今天下午搬走。他看上去差不多痊愈了。”

“是啊。”西比尔点点头。“他父亲打算试着叫他开始做事了。”

===Original===

“He seems very delicate,” said Mrs. Vertrees. “I shouldn’t think he would be able to stand a great deal, either physically or﻿—” She paused and then added, glowing with the sense of her own adroitness﻿—“or mentally.”

“Oh, mentally Bibbs is all right,” said Sibyl, in an odd voice.

“Entirely?” Mrs. Vertrees asked, breathlessly.

“Yes, entirely.”

===Chinese===

“他看着很单薄，”弗特里斯太太说。“我想他经不起多少折腾，不论身子骨上，还是——”她顿了顿，随即补上一句，为自己这份机巧心头直发热——“还是在脑筋上。”

“哦，比布斯的脑筋没毛病，”西比尔说，声音有点怪。

“完全没毛病？”弗特里斯太太屏着气问。

“是的，完全。”

===Original===

“But has he *always* been?” This question came with the same anxious eagerness.

“Certainly. He had a long siege of nervous dyspepsia, but he’s over it.”

“And you think﻿—”

“Bibbs is all right. You needn’t wor﻿—” Sibyl choked, and pressed her handkerchief to her mouth. “Good night, Mrs. Vertrees,” she said, hurriedly, as the headlights of an automobile swung round the corner above, sending a brightening glare toward the edge of the pavement where the two ladies were standing.

===Chinese===

“可他向来都这样没毛病吗？”这一问还是那么急切不安。

“当然。他闹过一场挺长的神经性消化不良，如今好了。”

“那您认为——”

“比布斯没毛病。您不必忧——”西比尔话堵在喉咙里，把手绢按到嘴上。“晚安，弗特里斯太太，”她急急地说。这时一辆汽车的车头灯正从上首街角拐过来，一道越来越亮的强光，朝两个女人站立的那条便道边上射过来。

===Original===

“Won’t you come in?” urged Mrs. Vertrees, cordially, hearing the sound of a cheerful voice out of the darkness beyond the approaching glare. “Do! There’s Mary now, and she﻿—”

But Sibyl was halfway across the street. “No, thanks,” she called. “I hope she won’t miss her piano!” And she ran into her own house and plunged headlong upon a leather divan in the hall, holding her handkerchief over her mouth.

The noise of her tumultuous entrance was evidently startling in the quiet house, for upon the bang of the door there followed the crash of a decanter, dropped upon the floor of the dining-room at the end of the hall; and, after a rumble of indistinct profanity, Roscoe came forth, holding a dripping napkin in his hand.

===Chinese===

“进来坐坐不好吗？”弗特里斯太太热诚地挽留。她听见那片越来越近的亮光之外的黑暗里，传来一个欢快的人声。“来嘛！玛丽回来了，她——”

可是西比尔已经穿过了半条街。“不用了，多谢，”她高声说。“但愿她别舍不得她的钢琴！”她跑进自己家，一头扑倒在门厅的皮面长榻上，把手绢紧紧捂着嘴。

她这场天翻地覆的进门声，在安静的宅子里显然吓人一跳：门砰地一响，紧跟着是刻花玻璃酒瓶摔碎的声音——瓶子掉在门厅尽头的餐室地板上；一阵含混的咒骂滚过之后，罗斯科走了出来，手里攥着一块湿漉漉的餐巾。

===Original===

“What’s your excitement?” he demanded. “What do you find to go into hysterics over? Another death in the family?”

“Oh, it’s funny!” she gasped. “Those old frostbitten people! I guess *they*’*re* getting their comeuppance!” Lying prone, she elevated her feet in the air, clapped her heels together repeatedly, in an ecstasy.

“Come through, come through!” said her husband, crossly. “What you been up to?”

===Chinese===

“你激动个什么劲？”他喝问。“你找着什么值得歇斯底里的事了？家里又死了人吗？”

“哦，笑死人了！”她喘着说。“那两个老冻骨头！我看他们的报应到——到了！”她直挺挺趴着，双脚翘到半空，两只鞋跟翻来覆去地磕碰，乐得神魂颠倒。

“有话快说，快说！”她丈夫没好气地说。“你又捣鼓了什么名堂？”

===Original===

“Me?” she cried, dropping her feet and swinging around to face him. “Nothing. It’s them! Those Vertreeses!” She wiped her eyes. “They’ve had to sell their piano!”

“Well, what of it?”

“That Mrs. Kittersby told me all about ’em a week ago,” said Sibyl. “They’ve been hard up for a long time, and she says as long ago as last winter she knew that girl got a pair of walking-shoes resoled and patched, because she got it done the same place Mrs. Kittersby’s cook had *hers*! And the night of the housewarming I kind of got suspicious, myself. She didn’t have one single piece of any kind of real jewelry, and you could see her dress was an old one done over. Men can’t tell those things, and you all made a big fuss over her, but I thought she looked a sight, myself! Of course, *Edith* was crazy to have her, and﻿—”

===Chinese===

“我？”她喊道，放下腿，一转身面对他。“我什么也没干。是他们！弗特里斯那家人！”她擦了擦笑出来的眼泪。“他们不得不把钢琴卖啦！”

“那又怎么样？”

“基特斯比太太一星期前就把他们家的底细都告诉我了，”西比尔说。“他们手头紧了好久，她说早在去年冬天就知道那位小姐拿一双便鞋去换过底、打过补丁，因为是在基特斯比太太的厨娘换她那双的同一家铺子办的！暖房宴那天晚上，我自己心里也起了点疑。她浑身上下没有一件真首饰，一眼看得出她那身衣裳是件改做的旧货。男人看不出这些门道，你们还围着她大惊小怪，我看她那副样子实在够呛！当然了，伊迪丝一门心思要请她来，还有——”

===Original===

“Well, well?” he urged, impatiently.

“Well, I’m *telling* you! Mrs. Kittersby says they haven’t got a *thing*! Just absolutely *nothing*﻿—and they don’t know anywhere to turn! The family’s all died out but them, and all the relatives they got are very distant, and live East and scarcely know ’em. She says the whole town’s been wondering what *would* become of ’em. The girl had plenty chances to marry up to a year or so ago, but she was so indifferent she scared the men off, and the ones that had wanted to went and married other girls. Gracious! they were lucky! Marry *her*? The man that found himself tied up to *that* girl﻿—”

“Terrible funny, terrible funny!” said Roscoe, with sarcasm. “It’s so funny I broke a cut-glass decanter and spilled a quart of﻿—”

===Chinese===

“后来呢？后来呢？”他不耐烦地催促。

“这不正告诉你嘛！基特斯比太太说他们一无所有！干干净净，一个子儿都没有——还走投无路！家里人死绝了就剩他们，亲戚全是八竿子打不着的远亲，都住在东部，跟他们几乎不认识。她说全城的人都在琢磨这家人往后可怎么得了。到一年多以前为止，这姑娘有的是出嫁的机会，可她那副爱答不理的架势把男人们都吓跑了，原先想娶她的那些个转头都娶了别的姑娘。老天！那帮人多走运！娶她？哪个男人要是发觉自己跟那个姑娘拴成了一对——”

“滑稽得很，滑稽得很！”罗斯科挖苦道。“可笑得我把一只刻花玻璃酒瓶都摔了，洒了一夸脱——”

===Original===

“Wait!” she begged. “You’ll see. I was sitting by the window a little while ago, and I saw a big wagon drive up across the street and some men go into the house. It was too dark to make out much, and for a minute I got the idea they were moving out﻿—the house has been foreclosed on, Mrs. Kittersby says. It seemed funny, too, because I knew that girl was out riding with Bibbs. Well, I thought I’d see, so I slipped over﻿—and it was their *piano*! They’d sold it and were trying to sneak it out after dark, so nobody’d catch on!” Again she gave way to her enjoyment, but resumed, as her husband seemed about to interrupt the narrative. “Wait a minute, can’t you? The old lady was superintending, and she gave it all away. I sized her up for one of those old churchy people that tell all kinds of lies except when it comes to so many words, and then they can’t. She might just as well told me outright! Yes, they’d sold it; and I hope they’ll pay some of their debts. They owe everybody, and last week a coal-dealer made an awful fuss at the door with Mr. Vertrees. Their cook told our upstairs girl, and she said she didn’t know *when* she’d seen any money, herself! Did you ever hear of such a case as that girl in your *life*?”

“What girl? Their cook?”

“That Vertrees girl! Don’t you see they looked on our coming up into this neighborhood as their last chance? They were just going down and out, and here bobs up the green, rich Sheridan family! So they doll the girl up in her old things, made over, and send her out to get a Sheridan﻿—she’s *got* to get one! And she just goes in blind; and she tries it on first with *you*. You remember, she just plain *told* you she was going to mash you, and then she found out you were the married one, and turned right square around to Jim and carried him off his feet. Oh, Jim was landed﻿—there’s no doubt about *that*! But Jim was lucky; he didn’t live to *stay* landed, and it’s a good thing for him!” Sibyl’s mirth had vanished, and she spoke with virulent rapidity. “Well, she couldn’t get you, because you were married, and she couldn’t get Jim, because Jim died. And there they were, dead broke! Do you know what she did? Do you know what she’s *doing*?”

===Chinese===

“等等！”她央求道。“你听下去就明白了。刚才一小会儿以前，我正坐在窗边，看见对面街上驶来一辆大货车，几个人进了那栋房子。天太黑，看不大真切，有一阵我还当他们要搬家呢——房子已经被抵债收走了，基特斯比太太说的。这事也显得蹊跷，因为我知道那姑娘出门跟比布斯兜风去了。于是我想过去看个究竟，就溜了过去——原来是他们家的钢琴！琴已经卖了，他们想趁着天黑偷偷运出去，免得叫人看破！”她说到这里又乐得直不起腰，可瞧她丈夫像要打断她的故事，又接了下去。“等一分钟行不行？那老太太亲自在旁边督工，把一切都露了馅。我一打量就断定，她是那种老教会气的教徒：什么谎都撒得出口，唯独到了要把话一个字一个字说白的当口，就撒不成了。她干脆直说给我听还不是一样！是的，琴是他们卖的；但愿他们能拿这钱还上几笔债。他们见谁都欠着，上星期有个煤商上门，当着弗特里斯先生大闹了一场。他们家的厨娘告诉了咱们楼上的丫环，她说她自己都不记得哪一回见过现钱了！你这辈子可听说过哪个姑娘像她这么个做法？”

“哪个姑娘？他们家的厨娘？”

“就是弗特里斯家那个姑娘！你还不明白吗，他们把咱们搬进这个街区，当成他们最后一根救命稻草？眼看就要一败涂地了，冷不丁冒出谢里丹家这么一门人傻钱多的阔亲戚！于是他们把姑娘用改做过的旧衣裳打扮起来，打发她出去钓一个谢里丹——她非钓着一个不可！她蒙着眼就往里闯；先拿你来试手。你记得的，她当时明明白白告诉过你，她要把你迷住；后来发觉你是有妇之夫，就干脆利索地掉头扑向吉姆，把他迷得神魂颠倒。哼，吉姆是上钩了——这一点没什么可怀疑的！不过吉姆运气好；他没能活着一直挂着钩，这对他倒是件好事！”西比尔的笑意已经褪尽，说话又急又毒。“这么着，你是有妇之夫，她钓不着你；吉姆死了，她又钓不着吉姆。一家人落得个身无分文！你知道她干了什么？你知道她现在干的是什么？”

===Original===

“No, I don’t,” said Roscoe, gruffly.

Sibyl’s voice rose and culminated in a scream of renewed hilarity. “*Bibbs!* She waited in the graveyard, and drove home with him from *Jim’s funeral*! Never spoke to him before! Jim wasn’t *cold*!”

She rocked herself back and forth upon the divan. “Bibbs!” she shrieked. “Bibbs! Roscoe, *think* of it! *Bibbs!*”

===Chinese===

“不知道，我不知道，”罗斯科粗声粗气地说。

西比尔的声音越拔越高，最后化作重新爆发的狂笑，尖叫起来。“比布斯！她在墓园里候着，从吉姆的葬礼上跟人家同车回家！从前连一句话都没跟他说过！吉姆尸骨未寒！”

她在长榻上前仰后合地摇。“比布斯！”她尖叫。“比布斯！罗斯科，你想想！比布斯！”

===Original===

He stared unsympathetically, but her mirth was unabated for all that. “And yesterday,” she continued, between paroxysms﻿—“yesterday she came out of the house﻿—just as he was passing. She must have been looking out﻿—waiting for the chance; I saw the old lady watching at the window! And she got him there last night﻿—to ‘*play*’ to him; the old lady gave that away! And today she made him take her out in a machine! And the cream of it is that they didn’t even know whether he was *insane* or not﻿—they thought maybe he was, but she went after him just the same! The old lady set herself to pump me about it today. *Bibbs!* Oh, my Lord! *Bibbs!*”

But Roscoe looked grim. “So it’s funny to you, is it? It sounds kind of pitiful to me. I should think it would to a woman, too.”

“Oh, it might,” she returned, sobering. “It might, if those people weren’t such frozen-faced smart Alecks. If they’d had the decency to come down off the perch a little I probably wouldn’t think it was funny, but to see ’em sit up on their pedestal all the time they’re eating dirt﻿—well, I think it’s funny! That girl sits up as if she was Queen Elizabeth, and expects people to wallow on the ground before her until they get near enough for her to give ’em a good kick with her old patched shoes﻿—oh, she’d do *that*, all right!﻿—and then she powders up and goes out to mash﻿—*Bibbs Sheridan!*”

===Chinese===

他冷冰冰地干瞪着眼，可她的狂笑一点不见收敛。“还有昨天，”她在一阵紧似一阵的笑声间歇里接着说——“昨天她从家里出来——正赶上他打门前走过。她准是一直往外瞧着——专等这个机会；我亲眼看见老太太在窗口守着！昨晚她把人弄到家里——给他‘弹琴’听；这是老太太自己露的馅！今天她又逼着人家开汽车带她出去！最妙的是，他们连他疯没疯都不知道——他们猜想他八成是疯了，可她照样扑了上去！今天老太太还专门拿话套我来着。比布斯！哦，我的老天爷！比布斯！”

可罗斯科脸色阴沉。“这么说你觉得好笑，是吧？我听着倒觉得有点可怜。我还当女人家也会这么觉得呢。”

“哦，说不定会，”她应道，收敛了几分。“说不定会，倘若那家人不是那么一副冻脸子还自命不凡的话。倘若他们稍微识相一点，肯从高枝上下来半步，我大概也不会觉得好笑；可是眼看着他们一边啃着泥，一边端坐在台座上——嘿，我就觉得好笑！那个姑娘端着架子，俨然伊丽莎白女王再世，专等众人先在她面前的地上打滚匍匐，滚得够近了，好叫她抡起那双打补丁的旧鞋结结实实踢上一脚——哦，这种事她干得出来，准的！——然后再扑扑粉，出门勾人去——勾比布斯·谢里丹！”

===Original===

“Look here,” said Roscoe, heavily; “I don’t care about that one way or another. If you’re through, I got something I want to talk to you about. I was going to, that day just before we heard about Jim.”

At this Sibyl stiffened quickly; her eyes became intensely bright. “What is it?”

“Well,” he began, frowning, “what I was going to say then﻿—” He broke off, and, becoming conscious that he was still holding the wet napkin in his hand, threw it pettishly into a corner. “I never expected I’d have to say anything like this to anybody I *married*; but I was going to ask you what was the matter between you and Lamhorn.”

===Chinese===

“听我说，”罗斯科沉甸甸地说。“这个我不感兴趣，怎么样都不感兴趣。你要是说完了，我有件事要跟你谈。本来打算那天谈的，就在咱们听说吉姆的事之前。”

一听这话西比尔立刻僵住了；她的眼睛亮得吓人。“什么事？”

“嗯，”他皱着眉头开了口，“我那天要说的是——”他忽然顿住，发觉自己手里还攥着那块湿餐巾，便赌气把它甩到墙角。“我从没想到，对自己娶来的人还得说这种话；可是我本来要问你，你和拉姆霍恩之间到底是怎么回事。”

===Original===

Sibyl uttered a sharp monosyllable. “Well?”

“I felt the time had come for me to know about it,” he went on. “You never told me anything﻿—”

“You never asked,” she interposed, curtly.

===Chinese===

西比尔从喉咙里挤出一个又尖又短的单音。“所以呢？”

“我觉得到了我该知道的时候了，”他接着说。“你从来没跟我说过——”

“你也从来没问过，”她截住话头，干巴巴地说。

===Original===

“Well, we’d got in a way of not talking much,” said Roscoe. “It looks to me now as if we’d pretty much lost the run of each other the way a good many people do. I don’t say it wasn’t my fault. I was up early and down to work all day, and I’d come home tired at night, and want to go to bed soon as I’d got the paper read﻿—unless there was some good musical show in town. Well, you seemed all right until here lately, the last month or so, I began to see something was wrong. I couldn’t help seeing it.”

“Wrong?” she said. “What like?”

“You changed; you didn’t look the same. You were all strung up and excited and fidgety; you got to looking peakid and run down. Now then, Lamhorn had been going with us a good while, but I noticed that not long ago you got to picking on him about every little thing he did; you got to quarreling with him when I was there and when I wasn’t. I could see you’d been quarreling whenever I came in and he was here.”

===Chinese===

“唉，咱们俩是落成了不大说话的习惯，”罗斯科说。“如今看来，咱们差不多是像好些人那样，把彼此给跟丢了。我不是说那不是我的错。我一早起来，整天在外做事，晚上拖着疲乏回家，报纸一读完就想上床——除非城里来了像样的歌舞戏。嗯，你瞧着一直好好的，直到近来这一把月，我开始看出不对劲来。想不看都不行。”

“不对劲？”她说。“怎么个不对法？”

“你变了，人不像原先那个人了。你整天绷得紧紧的，又兴奋又坐立不安；脸色越来越憔悴，整个人垮了下来。再说，拉姆霍恩跟咱们来往好久了，可我留意到，不久以前起，他干什么你都挑刺；有我在场你们吵，我不在场你们也吵。每回我进门只要他在，我都看得出你们刚吵过。”

===Original===

“Do you object to that?” asked Sibyl, breathing quickly.

“Yes﻿—when it injures my wife’s health!” he returned, with a quick lift of his eyes to hers. “You began to run down just about the time you began falling out with him.” He stepped close to her. “See here, Sibyl, I’m going to know what it means.”

“Oh, you *are*?” she snapped.

===Chinese===

“这也碍着你了吗？”西比尔问，呼吸急促。

“碍着——碍着它毁我老婆的身子！”他回敬道，眼睛飞快地一抬，迎上她的目光。“你开始垮，正是在你开始跟他闹翻的当口。”他逼近一步。“你听着，西比尔，我要弄个水落石出。”

“哦，你要弄？”她厉声说。

===Original===

“You’re trembling,” he said, gravely.

“Yes. I’m angry enough to do more than tremble, you’ll find. Go on!”

“That was all I was going to say the other day,” he said. “I was going to ask you﻿—”

===Chinese===

“你在发抖，”他板着脸说。

“是的。你往后会知道，我的火气大得不止发抖。接着说！”

“那天我要说的就是这些，”他说。“我本来要问你——”

===Original===

“Yes, that was all you were going to say *the other day*. Yes. What else have you to say tonight?”

“Tonight,” he replied, with grim swiftness, “I want to know why you keep telephoning him you want to see him since he stopped coming here.”

She made a long, low sound of comprehension before she said, “And what else did Edith want you to ask me?”

===Chinese===

“对，那是你那天要说的全部。对。今晚你还有什么要说的？”

“今晚，”他回答，又冷又快，“我要知道，他不登门以后，你为什么还一个劲儿打电话说你要见他。”

她拖长声音低低地“哦”了一声，恍然大悟似的，这才开口：“伊迪丝还叫你问什么？”

===Original===

“I want to know what you say over the telephone to Lamhorn,” he said, fiercely.

“Is that all Edith told you to ask me? You saw her when you stopped in there on your way home this evening, didn’t you? Didn’t she tell you then what I said over the telephone to Mr. Lamhorn?”

“No, she didn’t!” he vociferated, his voice growing louder. “She said, ‘You tell your wife to stop telephoning Robert Lamhorn to come and see her, because he isn’t going to do it!’ That’s what she said! And I want to know what it means. I intend﻿—”

===Chinese===

“我要知道你在电话里跟拉姆霍恩都说些什么，”他凶狠狠地说。

“伊迪丝就叫你问这个？今晚你回家路上在他们家落脚，见过她了，是不是？她当时没告诉你我在电话里对拉姆霍恩先生说了什么吗？”

“没有，她没说！”他嚷起来，声音越来越大。“她说：‘你去告诉你老婆，叫她别再打电话叫罗伯特·拉姆霍恩来见她，因为他不会来的！’她就是这么说的！我要知道这里头是怎么回事。我打算——”

===Original===

A maid appeared at the lower end of the hall. “Dinner is ready,” she said, and, giving the troubled pair one glance, went demurely into the dining-room. Roscoe disregarded the interruption.

“I intend to know exactly what has been going on,” he declared. “I mean to know just what﻿—”

Sibyl jumped up, almost touching him, standing face to face with him.

===Chinese===

一个女仆出现在门厅下首。“饭备好了，”她说，朝这对剑拔弩张的夫妻瞥了一眼，便端端正正地进了餐室。罗斯科没理会这个打岔。

“我打算把发生过的事一五一十弄清楚，”他宣布。“我就要弄清到底——”

西比尔蹦了起来，几乎撞上他，跟他脸对脸站着。

===Original===

“Oh, you *do*!” she cried, shrilly. “You mean to know just what’s what, do you? You listen to your sister insinuating ugly things about your wife, and then you come home making a scene before the servants and humiliating me in their presence! Do you suppose that Irish girl didn’t hear every word you said? You go in there and eat your dinner alone! Go on! Go and eat your dinner alone﻿—because *I* won’t eat with you!”

And she broke away from the detaining grasp he sought to fasten upon her, and dashed up the stairway, panting. He heard the door of her room slam overhead, and the sharp click of the key in the lock.

===Chinese===

“哦，你要弄清楚！”她尖叫。“你就要把什么是什么弄个明白，是吧？你听任你妹妹含沙射影，编排你老婆的不是，回到家来又当着下人的面大吵大闹，叫他们在场看我丢人！你以为那个爱尔兰丫头没把你说的每个字都听进耳朵？你自己进去，一个人吃你的饭去！去呀！一个人吃你的饭去——因为我不跟你吃！”

她挣脱了他想抓牢她的手，气喘吁吁地冲上楼梯。他听见楼上她的房门砰地关上，钥匙在锁孔里咔哒一响，又尖又脆。

## XVIII / 十八

===Original===
At seven o’clock on the last morning of that month, Sheridan, passing through the upper hall on his way to descend the stairs for breakfast, found a couple of scribbled sheets of notepaper lying on the floor. A window had been open in Bibbs’s room the evening before; he had left his notebook on the sill﻿—and the sheets were loose. The door was open, and when Bibbs came in and closed it, he did not notice that the two sheets had blown out into the hall. Sheridan recognized the handwriting and put the sheets in his coat pocket, intending to give them to George or Jackson for return to the owner, but he forgot and carried them downtown with him. At noon he found himself alone in his office, and, having a little leisure, remembered the bits of manuscript, took them out, and glanced at them. A glance was enough to reveal that they were not epistolary. Sheridan would not have read a private letter that came into his possession in that way, though in a matter of business he might have felt it his duty to take advantage of an opportunity afforded in any manner whatsoever. Having satisfied himself that Bibbs’s scribblings were only a sample of the kind of writing his son preferred to the machine-shop, he decided, innocently enough, that he would be justified in reading them.

===Chinese===
那个月的最后一个早晨，七点钟，谢里丹穿过楼上的走廊，正要下楼去吃早饭，看见地上躺着几张潦草写就的便笺。头一天傍晚，比布斯房里有一扇窗没关；他把笔记本搁在窗台上——本子里的纸页是散的。房门也开着，比布斯进来关上门的时候，没有察觉那两张纸叫风吹到了走廊里。谢里丹认得这笔迹，便把纸页塞进外衣口袋，打算交给乔治或是杰克逊送还失主，可他一忙就忘了，把纸页带进了城里。晌午时分，他发觉独自一人在办公室里，稍得一点闲暇，便想起那几页手稿，掏出来扫了一眼。一眼就看得出那不是书信。倘若一封私信这样落进他手里，谢里丹绝不会去读；可倘若是买卖上的事，那无论机会以何种方式送上门来，他兴许都会觉得加以利用是他的本分。他确认了比布斯这些涂鸦，不过是儿子拿它当作比机器车间中意的那路文章的一个样品之后，便心安理得地断定：自己读一读是说得过去的。


===Original===
> It appears that a lady will nod pleasantly upon some windy generalization of a companion, and will wear the most agreeable expression of accepting it as the law, and then﻿—days afterward, when the thing is a mummy to its promulgator﻿—she will inquire out of a clear sky: “*Why* did you say that the people downtown have nothing in life that a chicken hasn’t? What did you mean?” And she may say it in a manner that makes a sensible reply very difficult﻿—you will be so full of wonder that she remembered so seriously.

> Yet, what does the rooster lack? He has food and shelter; he is warm in winter; his wives raise not one fine family for him, but dozens. He has a clear sky over him; he breathes sweet air; he walks in his April orchard under a roof of flowers. He must die, violently perhaps, but quickly. Is Midas’s cancer a better way? The rooster’s wives and children must die. Are those of Midas immortal? His life is shorter than the life of Midas, but Midas’s life is only a sixth as long as that of the Galapagos tortoise.

> The worthy money-worker takes his vacation so that he may refresh himself anew for the hard work of getting nothing that the rooster doesn’t get. The office-building has an elevator, the rooster flies up to the bough. Midas has a machine to take him to his work; the rooster finds his worm underfoot. The business man feels a pressure sometimes, without knowing why, and sits late at wine after the day’s labor; next morning he curses his head because it interferes with the work﻿—he swears never to relieve that pressure again. The rooster has no pressure and no wine; this difference is in his favor.

===Chinese===
看样子，一位女士会听了同伴某句空泛的泛论而和颜悦色地点头，会摆出最可意的神情，把它当作金科玉律接受下来；然后——过了好些天，等那话在说它的人心里已经成了一具木乃伊——她会没头没脑地发问：“*你*为什么要说，城里的人过日子，没有一样东西是一只鸡所没有的？你这话是什么意思？”而她说这话的腔调，可能叫你很难得体地作答——你会满心诧异，诧异她竟记得这样认真。

可是，公鸡缺了什么呢？它有吃的，有住的；冬天它暖和；它的妻妾替它生儿育女，养的不是一门好家小，而是几十门。它头顶上有一片晴空；它呼吸甜美的空气；它在四月天的果园里散步，头顶一顶花的华盖。它终归要死，也许是横死，但死得快。难道迈达斯（Midas）的毒瘤就是一条更好的活路？公鸡的妻儿总归要死。迈达斯的妻儿就不死吗？公鸡的寿命比迈达斯短，可迈达斯的寿命，也只有加拉帕戈斯巨龟（Galapagos tortoise）的六分之一。

那位可敬的挣钱匠人去休假，为的是养足精神，好再来干那件苦差事：去挣那些公鸡不挣照样到手的东西。办公大厦有电梯，公鸡一飞就上了树梢。迈达斯有一台机器送他上工；公鸡的虫子就在脚底下。生意人有时候觉得心口憋得慌，又不知道为什么，一天的劳碌过后便迟迟坐着喝酒；第二天早上他咒骂自己的脑袋，因为它碍了活计——他赌咒发誓，往后再也不去松那一口憋闷。公鸡没有憋闷，也没有酒；这笔账算下来，占便宜的是公鸡。


===Original===
> The rooster is a dependent; he depends upon the farmer and the weather. Midas is a dependent; he depends upon the farmer and the weather. The rooster thinks only of the moment; Midas provides for tomorrow. What does he provide for tomorrow? Nothing that the rooster will not have without providing.

> The rooster and the prosperous worker: they are born, they grub, they love; they grub and love grubbing; they grub and they die. Neither knows beauty; neither knows knowledge. And after all, when Midas dies and the rooster dies, there is one thing Midas has had and rooster has not. Midas has had the excitement of accumulating what he has grubbed, and that has been his life and his love and his god. He cannot take that god with him when he dies. I wonder if the worthy gods are those we can take with us.

> Midas must teach all to be as Midas; the young must be raised in his religion﻿—

===Chinese===
公鸡是个寄食者；它靠农夫和天时养活。迈达斯也是个寄食者；他靠农夫和天时养活。公鸡只顾眼前一时；迈达斯为明天作打算。他为明天打算了什么呢？没打算出一样公鸡不打算就得不到的东西。

公鸡和发迹的挣饭人：他们生下来，刨食，恋爱；他们刨食，爱的是刨食；他们刨食，然后死去。谁也不识得美；谁也不识得知识。说到底，等到迈达斯死了、公鸡死了，有一件事迈达斯有过而公鸡没有。迈达斯尝过积攒刨来之物的那份兴头，那便是他的生涯、他的所爱、他的神。他死的时候，带不走那位神。我倒想知道，带得走的神，是不是才算可敬的神。

迈达斯必定要教人人都学迈达斯；年轻人必须在他的教门里养大——


===Original===
The manuscript ended there, and Sheridan was not anxious for more. He crumpled the sheets into a ball, depositing it (with vigor) in a wastebasket beside him; then, rising, he consulted a Cyclopedia of Names, which a book-agent had somehow sold to him years before; a volume now first put to use for the location of “Midas.” Having read the legend, Sheridan walked up and down the spacious office, exhaling the breath of contempt. “Dam’ fool!” he mumbled. But this was no new thought, nor was the contrariness of Bibbs’s notes a surpise to him; and presently he dismissed the matter from his mind.

He felt very lonely, and this was, daily, his hardest hour. For a long time he and Jim had lunched together habitually. Roscoe preferred a club luncheon, but Jim and his father almost always went to a small restaurant near the Sheridan Building, where they spent twenty minutes in the consumption of food, and twenty in talk, with cigars. Jim came for his father every day, at five minutes after twelve, and Sheridan was again in his office at five minutes before one. But now that Jim no longer came, Sheridan remained alone in his office; he had not gone out to lunch since Jim’s death, nor did he have anything sent to him﻿—he fasted until evening.

It was the time he missed Jim personally the most﻿—the voice and eyes and handshake, all brisk and alert, all businesslike. But these things were not the keenest in Sheridan’s grief; his sense of loss went far deeper. Roscoe was dependable, a steady old wheel-horse, and that was a great comfort; but it was in Jim that Sheridan had most happily perceived his own likeness. Jim was the one who would have been surest to keep the great property growing greater, year by year. Sheridan had fallen asleep, night after night, picturing what the growth would be under Jim. He had believed that Jim was absolutely certain to be one of the biggest men in the country. Well, it was all up to Roscoe now!

===Chinese===
手稿到这里就完了，谢里丹并不急着要看下文。他把那几张纸揉成一团，用力（着实使了不少劲）丢进身旁的废纸篓；随后站起身，去查一部《人名大辞典》（Cyclopedia of Names）——那是好些年前一个兜售书刊的贩子不知怎么推销给他的；这卷书头一回派上用场，为的是查“迈达斯”。读罢那条传说，谢里丹在宽敞的办公室里踱来踱去，一口一口往外呼着轻蔑的气息。“该死的蠢货！”他咕哝道。可这并不是什么新念头，比布斯这些笔记的别扭劲儿也不叫他意外；不多时，他便把这事从心里撵了出去。

他觉得非常孤单，这每天都是他顶难熬的时辰。长久以来，他和吉姆惯常一起用午饭。罗斯科偏爱去俱乐部吃，吉姆和父亲却几乎总是到谢里丹大厦附近一家小饭馆去，在那儿花二十分钟吃东西，再花二十分钟一面抽雪茄一面谈话。吉姆每天十二点过五分来接父亲，一点差五分谢里丹又坐回办公室。如今吉姆不再来了，谢里丹便独自留在办公室里；吉姆死后他没出去吃过一回午饭，也不叫人往里送一点吃的——他一直空着肚子熬到晚上。

这是他最想念吉姆本人的时辰——那嗓音、那眼睛、那握手，全都爽利机警，全都是买卖人的做派。可这些还不是谢里丹悲恸里最锥心的地方；他的失落扎得深得多。罗斯科靠得住，是一匹拉套的老辕马，这已是极大的安慰；可在吉姆身上，谢里丹才最称心地照见了自己的影子。要把那份大家业一年一年越养越大，吉姆是顶有把握的那一个。一夜又一夜，谢里丹都是描想着吉姆当家之后家业会长成什么样才睡去的。他原相信吉姆十拿九稳会成为全国顶大的人物之一。好了，如今全看罗斯科的了！


===Original===
That reminded him of a question he had in mind to ask Roscoe. It was a question Sheridan considered of no present importance, but his wife had suggested it﻿—though vaguely﻿—and he had meant to speak to Roscoe about it. However, Roscoe had not come into his father’s office for several days, and when Sheridan had seen his son at home there had been no opportunity.

He waited until the greater part of his day’s work was over, toward four o’clock, and then went down to Roscoe’s office, which was on a lower floor. He found several men waiting for business interviews in an outer room of the series Roscoe occupied; and he supposed that he would find his son busy with others, and that his question would have to be postponed, but when he entered the door marked “R. C. Sheridan. Private,” Roscoe was there alone.

He was sitting with his back to the door, his feet on a windowsill, and he did not turn as his father opened the door.

===Chinese===
这又提醒了他一个心里存着要问罗斯科的问题。在谢里丹看来，这问题眼下并不紧要，可他妻子提起过——虽然提得含含糊糊——他也本打算跟罗斯科谈一谈。然而罗斯科已有好几天没进过父亲的办公室，谢里丹在家里见到儿子的当儿，又总没得着机会。

他一直等到一天的活计办完了大半，将近四点钟，才下楼到罗斯科的办公室去，那在下面一层楼。罗斯科占着的那一串房间，外间候见室里有好几个月人在等着洽谈买卖；他料想儿子一定正忙着见人，他的问题又得往后推了，可他一推开那扇标着“R. C. 谢里丹　私室”的门，罗斯科却独自一人在里面。

他背朝着门坐着，两只脚搭在窗台上，父亲推门进来，他也没有回身。


===Original===
“Some pretty good men out there waitin’ to see you, my boy,” said Sheridan. “What’s the matter?”

“Nothing,” Roscoe answered indistinctly, not moving.

“Well, I guess that’s all right, too. I let ’em wait sometimes myself! I just wanted to ask you a question, but I expect it’ll keep, if you’re workin’ something out in your mind!”

===Chinese===
“外头有几位挺像样的人物等着见你呢，我的孩子，”谢里丹说。“这是怎么了？”

“没什么，”罗斯科含含糊糊地答道，身子没有动。

“呃，我看那也没什么。我自己有时候也让他们等着！我本来就想问你一句话，不过我看不急，要是你正心里盘算着什么事的话！”


===Original===
Roscoe made no reply; and his father, who had turned to the door, paused with his hand on the knob, staring curiously at the motionless figure in the chair. Usually the son seemed pleased and eager when he came to the office. “You’re all right, ain’t you?” said Sheridan. “Not sick, are you?”

“No.”

Sheridan was puzzled; then, abruptly, he decided to ask his question. “I wanted to talk to you about that young Lamhorn,” he said. “I guess your mother thinks he’s comin’ to see Edith pretty often, and you known him longer’n any of us, so﻿—”

===Chinese===
罗斯科没有作声；已经转身朝门口去的父亲，一只手搭在门把上停住了，好奇地瞪着椅子里那个一动不动的身影。往常这儿子来到办公室，总是满脸欢喜、跃跃欲试的。“你没事吧，啊？”谢里丹说。“不是病了吧？”

“不是。”

谢里丹给弄糊涂了；随即，他忽然拿定主意，把那个问题问了出来。“我本来想跟你聊聊那个姓拉姆霍恩的年轻人，”他说。“我估摸你妈是觉得他来看伊迪丝来得忒勤了点，你认得他又比我们谁都久，所以就——”


===Original===
“I won’t,” said Roscoe, thickly﻿—“I won’t say a dam’ thing about him!”

Sheridan uttered an exclamation and walked quickly to a position near the window where he could see his son’s face. Roscoe’s eyes were bloodshot and vacuous; his hair was disordered, his mouth was distorted, and he was deathly pale. The father stood aghast.

===Chinese===
“我不说，”罗斯科口齿含混地说——“他我半个该死的字也不说！”

谢里丹惊叫了一声，快步走到窗前一个能看见儿子脸的地方。罗斯科两眼布满血丝，目光空茫茫的；头发散乱，嘴巴歪扭，脸色死白。做父亲的怔怔地站着。


===Original===
“By George!” he muttered. “*Roscoe!*”

“My name,” said Roscoe. “Can’ help that.”

“*Roscoe!*” Blank astonishment was Sheridan’s first sensation. Probably nothing in the world could have more amazed him than to find Roscoe﻿—the steady old wheel-horse﻿—in this condition. “How’d you *get* this way?” he demanded. “You caught cold and took too much for it?”

===Chinese===
“我的老天！”他喃喃道。“*罗斯科！*”

“是我的名，”罗斯科说。“这可没法子。”

“*罗斯科！*”谢里丹头一个感觉是茫然吃惊。普天之下恐怕再没有什么事，比看见罗斯科——家里那匹拉套的老辕马——弄成这副样子更叫他震惊的了。“你是*怎么*弄成这样的？”他喝问。“着了凉，又吃药吃过量了？”


===Original===
For reply Roscoe laughed hoarsely. “Yeuh! Cold! I been drinkun all time, lately. Firs’ you notice it?”

“By George!” cried Sheridan. “I *thought* I’d smelt it on you a good deal lately, but I wouldn’t ’a’ believed you’d take more’n was good for you. Boh! To see you like a common hog!”

===Chinese===
罗斯科嘶哑地大笑一声算是回答。“呃！凉！我不停价地喝呢，近来。才头一回瞧出来？”

“我的老天！”谢里丹叫道。“我*就琢磨*近来你身上酒气冲得很，可我怎么也不肯信你会喝过了头，喝出坏处来。呸！瞧你这副寻常猪猡的样子！”


===Original===
Roscoe chuckled and threw out his right arm in a meaningless gesture. “Hog!” he repeated, chuckling.

“Yes, a hog!” said Sheridan, angrily. “In business hours! I don’t object to anybody’s takin’ a drink if you wants to, out o’ business hours; nor, if a man keeps his work right up to the scratch, I wouldn’t be the one to baste him if he got good an’ drunk once in two, three years, maybe. It ain’t *my* way. I let it alone, but I never believed in forcin’ my way on a grown-up son in moral matters. I guess I was wrong! You think them men out there are waitin’ to talk business with a drunkard? You think you can come to your office and do business drunk? By George! I wonder how often this has been happening and me not on to it! I’ll have a look over your books tomorrow, and I’ll﻿—”

Roscoe stumbled to his feet, laughing wildly, and stood swaying, contriving to hold himself in position by clutching the back of the heavy chair in which he had been sitting.

===Chinese===
罗斯科吃吃地笑，右臂向外一抡，抡得没头没脑。“猪猡！”他重复了一遍，兀自吃吃地笑。

“对，一头猪猡！”谢里丹怒冲冲地说。“在办公时间里！下班之外，谁要想喝一口，只管去喝，我不管闲事；再说，一个人活计要是干得顶到点儿上，哪怕两三年里喝得烂醉一回，兴许我也轮不着去数落他。那不是*我*的路数。我不沾那个，可我从来不主张在德行的事上，把我的路数硬加在一个成年的儿子身上。我看我错了！你当外头那些人是等着跟一个醉鬼谈买卖的？你当你能醉着上办公室来办买卖？我的老天！我不知道这样已经有过多少回，而我一点没察觉！明天我要把你的账本查一遍，我要——”

罗斯科跌跌撞撞地站起来，狂笑着，晃晃悠悠地立着，一手死死抓住他方才坐的那把沉甸甸的椅子的椅背，才算撑住了架势。


===Original===
“Hoo﻿—hoorah!” he cried. “ ’S my principles, too. Be drunkard all you want to﻿—outside business hours. Don’ for Gossake le’n’thing innerfere business hours! Business! Thassit! You’re right, father. Drink! Die! L’everything go to hell, but *don’* let innerfere business!”

Sheridan had seized the telephone upon Roscoe’s desk, and was calling his own office, overhead. “Abercrombie? Come down to my son Roscoe’s suite and get rid of some gentlemen that are waitin’ there to see him in room two-fourteen. There’s Maples and Schirmer and a couple o’ fellows on the Kinsey business. Tell ’em something’s come up I have to go over with Roscoe, and tell ’em to come back day after tomorrow at two. You needn’t come in to let me know they’re gone; we don’t want to be disturbed. Tell Pauly to call my house and send Claus down here with a closed car. We may have to go out. Tell him to hustle, and call me at Roscoe’s room as soon as the car gets here. ’T’s all!”

Roscoe had laughed bitterly throughout this monologue. “Drunk in business hours! Thass awf’l! Mus’n’ do such thing! Mus’n’ get drunk, mus’n’ gamble, mus’n’ kill ’nybody﻿—not in business hours! All right any other time. Kill ’nybody you want to﻿—’s long ’tain’t in business hours! Fine! Mus’n’ have any trouble ’t’ll innerfere business. Keep your trouble ’t home. Don’ bring it to th’ office. Might innerfere business! Have funerals on Sunday﻿—might innerfere business! Don’ let your wife innerfere business! Keep all, all, *all* your trouble an’ your meanness, an’ your trad﻿—your tradegy﻿—keep ’em *all* for home use! If you got die, go on die ’t home﻿—don’ die round th’ office! Might innerfere business!”

===Chinese===
“呜——呼啦！”他喊道。“这也是我的原则来着。你要当酒鬼随你当——下了班再说。看在老天分上，千万别叫什么事耽误了办公时间！买卖！就是这个理！你说得对，爸爸。喝！死！让什么都见鬼去，就是*别*耽误买卖！”

谢里丹一把抓起罗斯科桌上的电话，往楼上自己的办公室打。“阿伯克龙比（Abercrombie）？下楼到我儿子罗斯科这套间来，把在二一四号房间等着见他的那几位先生打发掉。有梅普尔斯（Maples）、席尔默（Schirmer），还有金西（Kinsey）那桩买卖上的两个伙计。就说出了点事，我得跟罗斯科过一遍，叫他们后天两点再来。他们走了你不必进来回我；我们不想叫人打搅。叫保利（Pauly）往我家里挂个电话，打发克劳斯开一辆封闭式汽车下来。我们兴许得出去一趟。叫他抓紧，车一到就叫罗斯科的房间给我来电话。就这些！”

这一大篇独白从头到尾，罗斯科都在惨笑。“办公时间喝醉了！了不得！可干不得这种事！不得喝醉，不得赌钱，不得杀人——办公时间里不得！旁的时候都好说。你想杀谁杀谁——只要不在办公时间！好得很！不得有任何耽误买卖的麻烦。麻烦留在家里。别往办公室带。会耽误买卖的！出殡搁在礼拜天办——会耽误买卖的！别叫你老婆耽误买卖！把你所有的、所有的、*所有*的麻烦，还有你的下作，还有你的买卖——你的悲局——统统留着，*统统*留作家里专用！要死就回家死去——别死在办公室左右！会耽误买卖的！”


===Original===
Sheridan picked up a newspaper from Roscoe’s desk, and sat down with his back to his son, affecting to read. Roscoe seemed to be unaware of his father’s significant posture.

“You know wh’ I think?” he went on. “I think Bibbs only one the fam’ly any ’telligence at all. Won’ work, an’ di’n’ get married. Jim worked, an’ he got killed. I worked, an’ I got married. Look at me! Jus’ look at me, I ask you. Fine ’dustriss young business man. Look whass happen’ to me! Fine!” He lifted his hand from the sustaining chair in a deplorable gesture, and, immediately losing his balance, fell across the chair and caromed to the floor with a crash, remaining prostrate for several minutes, during which Sheridan did not relax his apparent attention to the newspaper. He did not even look round at the sound of Roscoe’s fall.

Roscoe slowly climbed to an upright position, pulling himself up by holding to the chair. He was slightly sobered outwardly, having progressed in the prostrate interval to a state of befuddlement less volatile. He rubbed his dazed eyes with the back of his left hand.

===Chinese===
谢里丹从罗斯科桌上抄起一张报纸，背朝儿子坐下，装出看报的样子。罗斯科似乎没有理会父亲这层意味深长的姿态。

“你知道我琢磨什么吗？”他接着说。“我看比布斯是家里头一个有点灵性的。不肯干活，也没娶亲。吉姆干了活，结果送了命。我干了活，也娶了亲。瞧瞧我！就请你瞧瞧我。好一位勤勤恳恳的年轻生意人。瞧我落了个什么下场！真好！”他把撑着身子的那只手从椅背上抬起来，做了一个惨不忍睹的手势，立刻失了平衡，扑倒在椅子上，又弹到地板上，砰的一声，趴了好几分钟没有起来；这工夫，谢里丹看报的假模假式一分也没有松。罗斯科摔倒的声音都没叫他回一回头。

罗斯科抓着椅子慢慢爬起来，恢复了直立的姿势。表面上他略略清醒了些——趴倒的那段工夫里，他的昏沉已经进到了一个不那么翻江倒海的阶段。他用左手背揉了揉发懵的眼睛。


===Original===
“What﻿—what you ask me while ago?” he said.

“Nothin’.”

“Yes, you did. What﻿—what was it?”

“Nothin’. You better sit down.”

===Chinese===
“方才——方才你问我什么来着？”他说。

“没问什么。”

“不，你问了。什么——什么来着？”

“没什么。你顶好坐下。”


===Original===
“You ask’ me what I thought about Lamhorn. You did ask me that. Well, I won’t tell you. I won’t say dam’ word ’bout him!”

The telephone-bell tinkled. Sheridan placed the receiver to his ear and said, “Right down.” Then he got Roscoe’s coat and hat from a closet and brought them to his son. “Get into this coat,” he said. “You’re goin’ home.”

“All ri’,” Roscoe murmured, obediently.

===Chinese===
“你问我我看拉姆霍恩怎么样。你的确问了我这个。呃，我不告诉你。他我一个该死的字也不说！”

电话铃叮叮地响了。谢里丹把听筒放到耳边，说：“这就下来。”然后他从壁橱里取了罗斯科的外衣和帽子，送到儿子跟前。“把这件外套穿上，”他说。“你回家去。”

“好嘞，”罗斯科乖乖地咕哝道。


===Original===
They went out into the main hall by a side door, not passing through the outer office; and Sheridan waited for an empty elevator, stopped it, and told the operator to take on no more passengers until they reached the ground floor. Roscoe walked out of the building and got into the automobile without lurching, and twenty minutes later walked into his own house in the same manner, neither he nor his father having spoken a word in the interval.

Sheridan did not go in with him; he went home, and to his own room without meeting any of his family. But as he passed Bibbs’s door he heard from within the sound of a cheerful young voice humming jubilant fragments of song:

===Chinese===
父子俩从一扇侧门出去，进了大楼的正厅，没有经过外间办公室；谢里丹等来一部空电梯，拦住它，吩咐开电梯的在他们到楼下之前不要再搭别人。罗斯科走出大楼，上汽车时身子一晃也没晃，二十分钟后走进自己家门还是这么稳当；这一路上，父子俩谁也没有说一个字。

谢里丹没有陪他进去；他自己回了家，径直进了自己的房间，没有碰上家里任何人。可他经过比布斯的房门时，听见里面一个年轻的、兴冲冲的嗓子，正欢天喜地地哼着一支歌的断片：


===Original===
> *Who* looks a mustang in the eye?﻿ ﻿…

 With a leap from the ground

 To the saddle in a bound.

 And away﻿—and away!

 Hi-yay!

===Chinese===
> *谁*敢直视野马的眼睛？……

 纵身一跃离了地

 一蹦上了马鞍。

 奔啊——奔啊！

 嗨——嗬！


===Original===
It was the first time in Sheridan’s life that he had ever detected any musical symptom whatever in Bibbs﻿—he had never even heard him whistle﻿—and it seemed the last touch of irony that the useless fool should be merry today.

To Sheridan it was Tom o’ Bedlam singing while the house burned; and he did not tarry to enjoy the melody, but went into his own room and locked the door.

===Chinese===
谢里丹有生以来，这是头一回在比布斯身上察觉出一点音乐的症候——他连口哨都没听他吹过——而这个废物蠢货偏偏在今天乐成这样，仿佛讽刺还要添上最后一笔。

在谢里丹听来，这就是房子着了火、贝德兰姆的汤姆（Tom o’ Bedlam，《李尔王》中装疯的疯丐）还在唱歌；他无意逗留欣赏这曲调，径自进了自己的房间，锁上了门。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
He emerged only upon a second summons to dinner, two hours later, and came to the table so white and silent that his wife made her anxiety manifest and was but partially reassured by his explanation that his lunch had “disagreed” with him a little.

Presently, however, he spoke effectively. Bibbs, whose appetite had become hearty, was helping himself to a second breast of capon from white-jacket’s salver. “Here’s another difference between Midas and chicken,” Sheridan remarked, grimly. “Midas can eat rooster, but rooster can’t eat Midas. I reckon you overlooked that. Midas looks to me like he had the advantage there.”

Bibbs retained enough presence of mind to transfer the capon breast to his plate without dropping it and to respond, “Yes﻿—he crows over it.”

Having returned his antagonists’s fire in this fashion, he blushed﻿—for he could blush distinctly now﻿—and his mother looked upon him with pleasure, though the reference to Midas and roosters was of course jargon to her. “Did you ever see anybody improve the way that child has!” she exclaimed. “I declare, Bibbs, sometimes lately you look right handsome!”

===Chinese===
直到两个钟头之后第二遍唤晚饭，他才露面；走到餐桌边时，他脸色煞白，一言不发，他妻子把心里的不安明白露了出来，听了他那句午饭吃得有点“不对付”的解释，也只算放下了一半的心。

不过不多时，他开了口，而且一开口就见效。食欲已经变得旺盛的比布斯，正从白外套的托盘里往自己盘里取第二块阉鸡脯。“这儿又是迈达斯跟鸡的一处不同，”谢里丹板着脸说。“迈达斯能吃公鸡，公鸡可吃不了迈达斯。我看这一层你就没顾上想。要我说，这上头迈达斯占着便宜。”

比布斯还保得住几分镇定，把那块鸡脯稳稳移到自己盘里，没有失手掉落，还回了一句：“是啊——公鸡还会在他头上打鸣呢。”

他就这样回了对手的一枪，跟着脸红了——因为他如今已能红得分明——他母亲喜滋滋地望着他，虽然那迈达斯和公鸡的名堂在她听来自然是行外语。“你们几时见过有谁像这孩子这样见好的！”她嚷道，“说真的，比布斯，近来有时候你看着可真俊！”

===Original===
“He’s got to be such a gadabout,” Edith giggled.

“I found something of his on the floor upstairs this morning, before anybody was up,” said Sheridan. “I reckon if people lose things in this house and expect to get ’em back, they better get up as soon as I do.”

“What was it he lost?” asked Edith.

“He knows!” her father returned. “Seems to me like I forgot to bring it home with me. I looked it over﻿—thought probably it was something pretty important, belongin’ to a busy man like him.” He affected to search his pockets. “What *did* I do with it, now? Oh yes! Seems to me like I remember leavin’ it down at the office﻿—in the wastebasket.”

===Chinese===
“他如今可成了个东游西逛的忙人啦，”伊迪丝咯咯地笑。

“今天早上，趁谁都还没起身，我在楼上地板上捡着他的一样东西，”谢里丹说。“我看，谁要在这宅子里丢了东西还想找回来，顶好起得跟我一样早。”

“他丢的是什么呀？”伊迪丝问。

“他自己知道！”她父亲回敬道。“好像是我忘了给捎回家来。我翻看过一遍——心想多半是顶要紧的物件，像他这样的大忙人的东西。”他装模作样地在口袋里摸索。“我如今到底把它*怎么*着了？哦，想起来了！好像记得把它撂在楼底下办公室里了——扔在废纸篓里。”

===Original===
“Good place for it,” Bibbs murmured, still red.

Sheridan gave him a grin. “Perhaps pretty soon you’ll be gettin’ up early enough to find things before I do!”

It was a threat, and Bibbs repeated the substance of it, later in the evening, to Mary Vertrees﻿—they had come to know each other that well.

“My time’s here at last,” he said, as they sat together in the melancholy gaslight of the room which had been denuded of its piano. That removal had left an emptiness so distressing to Mr. and Mrs. Vertrees that neither of them had crossed the threshold since the dark day; but the gaslight, though from a single jet, shed no melancholy upon Bibbs, nor could any room seem bare that knew the glowing presence of Mary. He spoke lightly, not sadly.

===Chinese===
“搁那儿正好，”比布斯低声咕哝，脸还红着。

谢里丹朝他咧嘴一笑。“兴许要不了多久，你就能起得够早，赶在我头里找着东西啦！”

这是一句威胁话；当天夜里晚些时候，比布斯把这话的大意向玛丽·弗特里斯学了一遍——他们俩已经相熟到这个地步了。

“我的期限到底到了，”他说。两人一同坐在那间已被搬走钢琴的屋子里，煤气灯光下一片凄清。钢琴一搬，撇下的空荡叫弗特里斯先生和太太难过之极，自那个晦暗的日子起，两位谁也不曾再跨过那道门槛；可那煤气灯虽然只点着一个灯口，照在比布斯身上却毫不凄清，而凡有玛丽焕发着光彩在座的地方，哪间屋子也不会显得空落。他说得轻快，并不哀伤。

===Original===
“Yes, it’s come. I’ve shirked and put off, but I can’t shirk and put off any longer. It’s really my part to go to him﻿—at least it would save my face. He means what he says, and the time’s come to serve my sentence. Hard labor for life, I think.”

Mary shook her head. “I don’t think so. He’s too kind.”

“You think my father’s *kind*?” And Bibbs stared at her.

“Yes. I’m sure of it. I’ve felt that he has a great, brave heart. It’s only that he has to be kind in his own way﻿—because he can’t understand any other way.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，到了。我一直躲，一直拖，可是躲不成也拖不成了。上他那儿去本来就该是我去——至少还能给我留点脸面。他说话是算数的，如今服刑的日子到了。依我看，是终身苦役。”

玛丽摇了摇头。“我不这么想。他这人太仁厚了。”

“你当我的父亲*仁厚*？”比布斯直瞪瞪地望着她。

“是的。我拿得准。我一直觉得，他有一颗伟大的、勇敢的心。只不过他的仁厚只能按他自己的方式来——因为旁的方式他理解不了。”

===Original===
“Ah yes,” said Bibbs. “If that’s what you mean by ‘kind’!”

She looked at him gravely, earnest concern in her friendly eyes. “It’s going to be pretty hard for you, isn’t it?”

“Oh﻿—self-pity!” he returned, smiling. “This has been just the last flicker of revolt. Nobody minds work if he likes the kind of work. There’d be no loafers in the world if each man found the thing that he could do best; but the only work I happen to want to do is useless﻿—so I have to give it up. Tomorrow I’ll be a day-laborer.”

“What is it like﻿—exactly?”

===Chinese===
“哦，是啊，”比布斯说，“要是这就算你说的‘仁厚’！”

她郑重地望着他，友好的眼睛里满含恳切的关切。“这一来你会很不好受的，是不是？”

“哦——不过是自怜自叹罢了！”他笑着回答。“这不过是反叛的最后一点残焰。人只要喜欢自己那份活计，就不嫌干活。倘若各人都寻得着自己顶拿手的事，世上就不会有无业游民；可我碰巧想干的唯一行当毫无用处——所以我只好舍了它。打明天起，我就是个打日工的了。”

“那活计到底什么样——确切地说？”

===Original===
“I get up at six,” he said. “I have a lunch-basket to carry with me, which is aristocratic and no advantage. The other workmen have tin buckets, and tin buckets are better. I leave the house at six-thirty, and I’m at work in my overalls at seven. I have an hour off at noon, and work again from one till five.”

“But the work itself?”

“It wasn’t muscularly exhausting﻿—not at all. They couldn’t give me a heavier job because I wasn’t good enough.”

“But what will you do? I want to know.”

===Chinese===
“我六点起床，”他说。“得随身带一只饭篮，这派头够贵族气，却一点好处没有。别的工人提的是洋铁桶，洋铁桶反倒更好。六点半出门，七点就穿着工装上工。中午歇一个钟头，一点再干到五点。”

“可那活儿本身呢？”

“倒不怎么累筋骨——一点儿也不。他们不能派我更重的活，因为我手艺不够好。”

“可你到底干的是什么？我要知道。”

===Original===
“When I left,” said Bibbs, “I was on what they call over there a ‘clipping-machine,’ in one of the ‘byproducts’ departments, and that’s what I’ll be sent back to.”

“But what is it?” she insisted.

Bibbs explained. “It’s very simple and very easy. I feed long strips of zinc into a pair of steel jaws, and the jaws bite the zinc into little circles. All I have to do is to see that the strip goes into the jaws at a certain angle﻿—and yet I was a very bad hand at it.”

He had kept his voice cheerful as he spoke, but he had grown a shade paler, and there was a latent anguish deep in his eyes. He may have known it and wished her not to see it, for he turned away.

===Chinese===
“我走的时候，”比布斯说，“我在那边他们管叫‘裁切机’的机器上做活，属一个‘副产品’部门；回头还是把我派回那儿去。”

“可那究竟是什么？”她追着问。

比布斯解释给她听。“极简单，也极容易。我把长长的锌条喂进一副钢牙中间，钢牙把锌咬成一个个小圆片。我要做的全部事情，就是看着锌条按一定的角度喂进钢牙——可我干这个偏偏是个顶糟的手。”

他说的时候声音一直强作欢快，可脸色已经又淡了一层，眼睛深处藏着一种隐伏的痛楚。他也许自己知道，不愿叫她看见，便把脸转开了。

===Original===
“You do that all day long?” she asked, and as he nodded, “It seems incredible!” she exclaimed. “*You* feeding a strip of zinc into a machine nine hours a day! No wonder﻿—” She broke off, and then, after a keen glance at his face, she said: “I should think you *would* have been a bad hand at it!”

He laughed ruefully. “I think it’s the noise, though I’m ashamed to say it. You see, it’s a very powerful machine, and there’s a sort of rhythmical crashing﻿—a crash every time the jaws bite off a circle.”

“How often is that?”

“The thing should make about sixty-eight disks a minute﻿—a little more than one a second.”

===Chinese===
“你就整天整天地干那个？”她问。他一点头，她便嚷起来：“简直叫人不肯相信！*你*，一天九个钟头往机器里喂锌条！怪不得——”她忽然住了口，把他的脸锐利地端详了一番，然后说：“要我说，你*当然*是个顶糟的手！”

他凄然一笑。“我想是那噪音的缘故，虽说这话说出来我自己也有点臊。你瞧，那是一台劲道很大的机器，有一种挺有节律的轰响——钢牙每咬下一个圆片，就轰响一声。”

“多久响一回？”

“那机器一分钟该出六十八个圆片——一秒钟略略多过一个。”

===Original===
“And you’re close to it?”

“Oh, the workman has to sit in its lap,” he said, turning to her more gaily. “The others don’t mind. You see, it’s something wrong with me. I have an idiotic way of flinching from the confounded thing﻿—I flinch and duck a little every time the crash comes, and I couldn’t get over it. I was a treat to the other workmen in that room; they’ll be glad to see me back. They used to laugh at me all day long.”

Mary’s gaze was averted from Bibbs now; she sat with her elbow resting on the arm of the chair, her lifted hand pressed against her cheek. She was staring at the wall, and her eyes had a burning brightness in them.

“It doesn’t seem possible anyone could do that to you,” she said, in a low voice. “No. He’s not kind. He ought to be proud to help you to the leisure to write books; it should be his greatest privilege to have them published for you﻿—”

===Chinese===
“你就守在它跟前？”

“哦，做工的只得坐在它怀里，”他说着朝她转过身来，神气更欢了。“别人都不在乎。你看，毛病出在我身上。我一见那该死的东西就犯一种傻子似的毛病——直往后缩；每回轰响一起来，我就缩一缩、躲一躲，怎么也改不掉。那一间屋里别的工人拿我当活宝；我回去他们准高兴。他们从前整天价笑我。”

这时玛丽的目光已从比布斯身上移开；她坐在那儿，臂肘支着椅子扶手，抬起的手紧紧贴着面颊。她瞪着墙壁，眼睛里燃着一种灼灼的亮光。

“居然有人能这样待你，这简直不像可能的事，”她低声说。“不。他不仁厚。他应当以助你得闲著书为荣；替你把它们付印，应当是他顶大的殊荣——”

===Original===
“Can’t you *see* him?” Bibbs interrupted, a faint ripple of hilarity in his voice. “If he could understand what you’re saying﻿—and if you can imagine his taking such a notion, he’d have had R. T. Bloss put up posters all over the country: ‘Read B. Sheridan. Read the Poet with a Punch!’ No. It’s just as well he never got the﻿—But what’s the use? I’ve never written anything worth printing, and I never shall.”

“You could!” she said.

“That’s because you’ve never seen the poor little things I’ve tried to do.”

“You wouldn’t let me, but I *know* you could! Ah, it’s a pity!”

===Chinese===
“你难道*看*不出他是个什么人？”比布斯打断她，嗓音里起了一丝笑的涟漪。“他即便能懂你说的话——就算你能设想他起了这么一个念头——他也早叫R·T·布洛斯把招贴贴遍全国了：‘读B·谢里丹。读一记重拳的诗人！’不会的。好在他也没拿到那——可这又有什么用处呢？我从来没写出过值得印的东西，往后也决不会。”

“你写得出来的！”她说。

“那是因为你从没见过我试着写过的那些可怜的小东西。”

“你不肯给我看，可我*知道*你行！唉，多可惜！”

===Original===
“It isn’t,” said Bibbs, honestly. “I never could﻿—but you’re the kindest lady in this world, Miss Vertrees.”

She gave him a flashing glance, and it was as kind as he said she was. “That sounds wrong,” she said, impulsively. “I mean ‘Miss Vertrees.’ I’ve thought of you by your first name ever since I met you. Wouldn’t you rather call me ‘Mary’?”

Bibbs was dazzled; he drew a long, deep breath and did not speak.

“Wouldn’t you?” she asked, without a trace of coquetry.

===Chinese===
“并不可惜，”比布斯老老实实地说。“我从来就写不出——可你是这世上顶仁厚的小姐，弗特里斯小姐。”

她明晃晃地瞟了他一眼，那一眼恰如他所说的，满是仁厚。“这听着不对，”她一时冲动地说。“我是说‘弗特里斯小姐’这个称呼。打认识你那天起，我心里一直用你的名字想你。你不情愿干脆叫我‘玛丽’么？”

比布斯被耀得眼花缭乱；他长长地、深深地吸了一口气，没有作声。

“你不情愿么？”她问，没有一丝卖弄。

===Original===
“If I *can*!” he said, in a low voice.

“Ah, that’s very pretty!” she laughed. “You’re such an honest person, it’s pleasant to have you gallant sometimes, by way of variety.” She became grave again immediately. “I hear myself laughing as if it were someone else. It sounds like laughter on the eve of a great calamity.” She got up restlessly, crossed the room and leaned against the wall, facing him. “You’ve *got* to go back to that place?”

He nodded.

“And the other time you did it﻿—”

===Chinese===
“只要我*能*！”他低声说。

“啊，这话真中听！”她笑了。“你这个人这样老实，偶尔殷勤一句，权当换换花样，倒叫人愉快。”她立刻又郑重起来。“我听见自己的笑声，倒像那是旁的什么人在笑。听着像大祸临头前夜的笑声。”她烦躁地立起身，穿过屋子，靠在墙上，面朝着他。“你*非*回那个地方去不可？”

他点了点头。

“那你上一回干那活的时候——”

===Original===
“Just over it,” said Bibbs. “Two years. But I don’t mind the prospect of a repetition so much as﻿—”

“So much as what?” she prompted, as he stopped.

Bibbs looked up at her shyly. “I want to say it, but﻿—but I come to a dead balk when I try. I﻿—”

“Go on. Say it, whatever it is,” she bade him. “You wouldn’t know how to say anything I shouldn’t like.”

===Chinese===
“刚刚缓过来，”比布斯说。“两年。可是再来一遍，我怕倒不怕，怕的是——”

“怕的是什么？”她见他住口，追了一句。

比布斯怯生生地抬头望她。“我想把它说出来，可是——可是一试就撞上死墙，进退不得。我——”

“说吧。不管是什么，说出来，”她吩咐他。“叫我听了不高兴的话，你根本不会说。”

===Original===
“I doubt if you’d either like or dislike what I want to say,” he returned, moving uncomfortably in his chair and looking at his feet﻿—he seemed to feel awkward, thoroughly. “You see, all my life﻿—until I met you﻿—if I ever felt like saying anything, I wrote it instead. Saying things is a new trick for me, and this﻿—well, it’s just this: I used to feel as if I hadn’t ever had any sort of a life at all. I’d never been of use to anything or anybody, and I’d never had anything, myself, except a kind of haphazard thinking.
