神殿本身的外头，还有那班冒牌人物：成群结伙的窃贼、骗子、剪羊毛的，鬼祟的无赖与公开的无赖；可这些都是孱弱之辈，一经识破便无足畏惧，何况他有一位好向导替他一一指点。他得知，这些人有时倒有用处，其中许多在买卖必须触及政界的事务里充当掮客。他还得知，酿酒公司、电车公司、银行以及别的机构如何彼此争斗，争夺这座城市的政治控制权。他发现，报纸早已失去旧日的政治势力，尤其在知情者中间，后者带着将信将疑的诙谐看待它们，认定这些报刊要么是像律师一样被豢养的党羽，要么就是在替主人的个人野心开路。城市的控制权并不在他们手里，通常是靠给成群的黑人塞杜松子酒钱，加上别的赏赐，方能到手。于是人民的岁入便尽可能公平地分发给一大批曾襄助得胜一方的人。许多彩头还附带名号与官职，而多数有衔者也被指望时不时摆出一副忙碌的模样；说实话，其中确有一些人做事诚实而尽心。
比布斯从前见识十分浅陋。所有这些寻常事，尽人皆知、习以为常，起初都叫他吃惊；有一回——在短短一瞬里忘了自己已经与写作断绝——他想，倘若他从前知道这些并且写过它们，哪怕最平淡的照实陈述，也必显得多像一部讽刺作品！在他最觉奇怪的，是那股猛烈而真挚的爱国心。他四面八方都听见它——它是一种浸染；最新入学的蒙童也沾上了它，尽管他刚从匈牙利来，本地话才学着结结巴巴讲上几句。到处都有人民在呐喊，喊他们的城市何等有权势、何等庞大、何等富足、何等蒸蒸日上。还不止此，他们说本城的人民是天下最伟大、最优秀、最强壮、最庞大的人民。他们不引什么权威，也觉得无须引证，因为他们自己就是那受颂扬的人民。倘若这件事遭到质疑，或者有人暗示在某一种小小的德性上他们未必完美至尊，他们也决不浪费时间省察自身、看看批评家的话是真是假，而是扑上去轰他、嘘他、咒他，因为他们敏感。于是比布斯，一边学他们的样子一边同他们同行，侧耳听从人民的声音，同他们一道供奉庞大。因为人民的声音，就是他们的神的声音。

---

===Original===

Sheridan had made the room next to his own into an office for Bibbs, and the door between the two rooms usually stood open﻿—the father had established that intimacy. One morning in February, when Bibbs was alone, Sheridan came in, some sheets of typewritten memoranda in his hand.
“Bibbs,” he said, “I don’t like to butt in very often this way, and when I do I usually wish I hadn’t﻿—but for Heaven’s sake what have you been buying that ole busted inter-traction stock for?”
Bibbs leaned back from his desk. “For eleven hundred and fifty-five dollars. That’s all it cost.”

===Chinese===

谢里丹把自己隔壁那间屋子腾出来，给比布斯做了办公室，两屋之间的门通常是敞开的——这层亲密是父亲定下的。二月里的一个早晨，屋里只有比布斯一人，谢里丹走了进来，手里拿着几页打字机打的备忘录。
“比布斯，”他说，“我不大喜欢老是这么闯进来插手，一插完手我通常就巴不得没插过——可是看老天爷面上，你买那种破了产的城际电车股票，究竟图的什么？”
比布斯从书桌前向后一仰。“一千一百五十五块钱。它就花了这么多。”

===Original===

“Well, it ain’t worth eleven hundred and fifty-five cents. You ought to know that. I don’t get your idea. That stuff’s deader’n Adam’s cat!”
“It might be worth something﻿—some day.”
“How?”
“It mightn’t be so dead﻿—not if we went into it,” said Bibbs, coolly.

===Chinese===

“哼，它连一千一百五十五分钱都不值，这个你该知道。我摸不透你的心思。那玩意儿死得比亚当的猫还要死透！”
“它说不定值几个钱——有一天。”
“怎么会？”
“它未必就那么死——只要咱们进去做，就不见得，”比布斯冷冷地说。

===Original===

“Oh!” Sheridan considered this musingly; then he said, “Who’d you buy it from?”
“A broker﻿—Fansmith.”
“Well, he must ’a’ got it from one o’ the crowd o’ poor ninnies that was soaked with it. Don’t you know who owned it?”
“Yes, I do.”

===Chinese===

“哦！”谢里丹若有所思地把这话琢磨了一阵；然后他说：“你打谁手里买的？”
“一个经纪人——范斯密斯（Fansmith）。”
“哼，他准是从那帮叫这玩意儿套牢的可怜糊涂虫手里收来的。你知不知道原先是谁的？”
“知道，我知道。”

===Original===

“Ain’t sayin’, though? That it? What’s the matter?”
“It belonged to Mr. Vertrees,” said Bibbs, shortly, applying himself to his desk.
“So!” Sheridan gazed down at his son’s thin face. “Excuse me,” he said. “Your business.” And he went back to his own room. But presently he looked in again.

===Chinese===

“可不肯说是吧？是这么回事吧？碍着什么了？”
“它原先是弗特里斯先生的，”比布斯没好气地说，低头去办桌上的事了。
“原来如此！”谢里丹低头端详儿子那张瘦脸。“失敬，”他说。“你的买卖。”说罢回自己屋里去了。可没过多大一会儿，他又探进头来。

===Original===

“I reckon you won’t mind lunchin’ alone today”﻿—he was shuffling himself into his overcoat﻿—“because I just thought I’d go up to the house and get *this* over with mamma.” He glanced apologetically toward his right hand as it emerged from the sleeve of the overcoat. The bandages had been removed, finally, that morning, revealing but three fingers﻿—the forefinger and the finger next to it had been amputated. “She’s bound to make an awful fuss, and better to spoil her lunch than her dinner. I’ll be back about two.”
But he calculated the time of his arrival at the New House so accurately that Mrs. Sheridan’s lunch was not disturbed, and she was rising from the lonely table when he came into the dining-room. He had left his overcoat in the hall, but he kept his hands in his trousers pockets.
“What’s the matter, papa?” she asked, quickly. “Has anything gone wrong? You ain’t sick?”

===Chinese===

“我估摸你今天不会介意一个人吃午饭”——他正往大衣里套——“我寻思不如上宅子里去，把*这桩事*跟妈妈了结掉。”他带着歉意瞟了一眼刚从大衣袖管里探出来的右手。绷带那天早上终于拆掉了，露出仅仅三根指头——食指和挨着它的那根都截去了。“她必定要大闹一场，宁可搅了她的午饭，也别搅了她的晚饭。我两点左右回来。”
不过他把到达新宅的时刻算得那么准，谢里丹太太的午饭竟丝毫没受搅扰，他走进餐室时，她正从独自就座的桌旁起身。他把大衣留在了门厅，两只手却始终插在裤袋里。
“怎么啦，爸爸？”她急忙问。“出了什么岔子？你莫不是病了？”

===Original===

“Me!” He laughed loudly. “Me *sick*?”
“You had lunch?”
“Didn’t want any today. You can give me a cup o’ coffee, though.”
She rang, and told George to have coffee made, and when he had withdrawn she said querulously, “I just know there’s something wrong.”

===Chinese===

“我！”他放声大笑。“我*病*？”
“你吃过午饭了？”
“今天不想吃。不过你可以给我来一杯咖啡。”
她摇铃唤来乔治，吩咐去备咖啡；他退出去之后，她悻悻然说：“我准知道有什么不对劲。”

===Original===

“Nothin’ in the world,” he responded, heartily, taking a seat at the head of the table. “I thought I’d talk over a notion o’ mine with you, that’s all. It’s more womenfolks’ business than what it is man’s, anyhow.”
“What about?”
“Why, ole Doc Gurney was up at the office this morning awhile﻿—”

===Chinese===

“天底下没事儿，”他一片至诚地应道，在餐桌主位上坐下。“我不过是想跟你合计合计我的一个念头，就这么回事。这反正更是娘儿们家的事，不算老爷们的事。”
“关于什么？”
“呃，今天早上老格尼大夫上办公室来待了一会儿——”

===Original===

“To look at your hand? How’s he say it’s doin’?”
“Fine! Well, he went in and sat around with Bibbs awhile﻿—”
Mrs. Sheridan nodded pessimistically. “I guess it’s time you had him, too. I *knew* Bibbs﻿—”

===Chinese===

“来看你的手？他说好得怎么样啦？”
“好得很！呃，他进去又陪着比布斯坐了一阵——”
谢里丹太太悲观地点点头。“我看也该轮到叫他瞧瞧比布斯了。我*早知道*比布斯——”

===Original===

“Now, mamma, hold your horses! I wanted him to look Bibbs over *before* anything’s the matter. You don’t suppose I’m goin’ to take any chances with *Bibbs*, do you? Well, afterwards, I shut the door, and I an’ ole Gurney had a talk. He’s a mighty disagreeable man; he rubbed it in on me what he said about Bibbs havin’ brains if he ever woke up. Then I thought he must want to get something out o’ me, he go so flattering﻿—for a minute! ‘Bibbs couldn’t help havin’ business brains,’ he says, ‘bein’ *your* son. Don’t be surprised,’ he says﻿—‘don’t be surprised at his makin’ a success,’ he says. ‘He couldn’t get over his heredity; he couldn’t *help* bein’ a business success﻿—once you got him into it. It’s in his blood. Yes, sir,’ he says, ‘it doesn’t need *much* brains,’ he says, ‘an only third-rate brains, at that,’ he says, ‘but it does need a special *kind* o’ brains,’ he says, ‘to be a millionaire. I mean,’ he says, ‘when a man’s given a start. If nobody gives him a start, why, course he’s got to have luck *and* the right kind o’ brains. The only miracle about Bibbs,’ he says, ‘is where he got the *other* kind o’ brains﻿—the brains you made him quit usin’ and throw away.’ ”

===Chinese===

“哎，妈妈，你先别急！我是要他趁*还没出事*先给比布斯查一查。你总不至于当我肯拿*比布斯*去冒险吧？呃，过后我关上门，我跟老格尼谈了一阵。他这人顶不讨人喜欢；把从前说的比布斯只要醒过来就有脑子那话，又拿来回我的脸。后来我寻思他准是想从我身上捞点什么，他捧得那么起劲——有那么一分钟！‘比布斯天生的就有做买卖的脑子，’他说，‘谁叫他是*你*的儿子。别奇怪，’他说——‘别奇怪他能成事，’他说。‘他摆脱不了他的遗传；只要你把他弄了进去，他*免不了*要在买卖上成事。这是他血里带来的。是的，先生，’他说，‘做百万富翁用不着*多*高的脑子，’他说，‘至多也不过是三流的脑子，’他说，‘可它确实得要一种特别的*种类*的脑子，’他说。‘我是说，’他说，‘在有人给他起好了头的时候。要是没人给他起头，那自然，他就得既有运气*又*有对路的那种脑子。比布斯身上唯一的奇迹，’他说，‘是那种*另外的*脑子他打哪儿来的——就是你叫他不许用、叫扔掉的那种。’”

===Original===

“But what’d he say about his health?” Mrs. Sheridan demanded, impatiently, as George placed a cup of coffee before her husband. Sheridan helped himself to cream and sugar, and began to sip the coffee.
“I’m comin’ to that,” he returned, placidly. “See how easy I manage this cup with my left hand, mamma?”
“You been doin’ that all winter. What did﻿—”

===Chinese===

“可讲到他的身体，他到底怎么说？”谢里丹太太不耐烦地追问，这时乔治把一杯咖啡放到她丈夫面前。谢里丹自己搀了奶油和糖，小口呷起咖啡来。
“这就讲到啦，”他心平气和地答。“瞧我用左手使唤这只杯子多利索，妈妈？”
“你一冬天都在这么使唤了。他到底说——”

===Original===

“It’s wonderful,” he interrupted, admiringly, “what a fellow can do with his left hand. I can sign my name with mine now, well’s I ever could with my right. It came a little hard at first, but now, honest, I believe I *rather* sign with my left. That’s all I ever have to write, anyway﻿—just the signature. Rest’s all dictatin’.” He blew across the top of the cup unctuously. “Good coffee, mamma! Well, about Bibbs. Ole Gurney says he believes if Bibbs could somehow get back to the state o’ mind he was in about the machine-shop﻿—that is, if he could some way get to feelin’ about business the way he felt about the shop﻿—not the poetry and writin’ part, but﻿—” He paused, supplementing his remarks with a motion of his head toward the old house next door. “He says Bibbs is older and harder’n what he was when he broke down that time, and besides, he ain’t the kind o’ dreamy way he was then﻿—and I should say he *ain’t*! I’d like ’em to show *me* anybody his age that’s any wider awake! But he says Bibbs’s health never need bother us again if﻿—”
Mrs. Sheridan shook her head. “I don’t see any help *that* way. You know yourself she wouldn’t have Jim.”

===Chinese===

“真是奇妙，”他打断她，一副自我赞叹的口气，“一个人的左手能派上多大用场。我这名字如今用左手签，跟从前用右手签得一样好。起头有点儿费劲，可如今，说真的，我倒*宁愿*用左手签。反正我这辈子要动的笔也就是签个名，其余的全是口述。”他油润地朝杯口上吹了一口气。“好咖啡，妈妈！呃，说比布斯。老格尼说，他相信要是比布斯能想法子回到他当年对待机器车间的那份心境——就是说，要是他能想个法子，叫自己对买卖生出当年对车间那样的心思——不是诗和写作那一部分，而是——”他顿住了，朝隔壁那座老宅那边偏了偏头，补足了他的话。“他说比布斯比上回垮掉那阵子年纪大了，也更硬了，再说，他也不是当年那副做梦的神气了——依我看他*断乎不是*！我倒想叫他们指*给我*看看，他那岁数的人里头，还有谁比他更醒得透！不过他说，比布斯的健康往后再不必咱们操心，只要——”
谢里丹太太摇头。“我看不出*那个法子*有什么指望。你自己也知道，她连吉姆都不肯要。”

===Original===

“Who’s talkin’ about her havin’ anybody? But, my Lord! she might let him *look* at her! She needn’t ’a’ got so mad, just because he asked her, that she won’t let him come in the house any more. He’s a mighty funny boy, and some ways I reckon he’s pretty near as hard to understand as the Bible, but Gurney kind o’ got me in the way o’ thinkin’ that if she’d let him come back and set around with her an evening or two sometimes﻿—not reg’lar, I don’t mean﻿—why﻿—Well, I just thought I’d see what *you’d* think of it. There ain’t any way to talk about it to Bibbs himself﻿—I don’t suppose he’d let you, anyhow﻿—but I thought maybe you could kind o’ slip over there some day, and sort o’ fix up to have a little talk with her, and kind o’ hint around till you see how the land lays, and ask her﻿—”
“*Me!*” Mrs. Sheridan looked both helpless and frightened. “No.” She shook her head decidedly. “It wouldn’t do any good.”

===Chinese===

“谁说她要谁了？可是，我的老天爷！她总该容他*看一看*她吧！他不过求了她一回，她就火到不许他再进家门，这也犯不上。他是个顶古怪的男孩，有几分我估摸着差不多跟《圣经》一样难懂，可格尼多少叫我觉得，要是她肯放他回来，偶尔陪她坐上一两个晚上——不是回回都来，我不是那个意思——那——呃，我就是想听听*你*对这事的看法。这事没法跟比布斯本人开口——我料着他也不容你对他说——不过我想，兴许你哪天能悄悄溜过去，安排着跟她小小谈一谈，绕着弯儿旁敲侧击，探明了形势，再问她——”
“*我*！”谢里丹太太一脸无助兼惊惶。“不行。”她断然摇头。“那不会有什么用处。”

===Original===

“You won’t try it?”
“I won’t risk her turnin’ me out o’ the house. Some way, that’s what I believe she did to Sibyl, from what Roscoe said once. No, I *can’t*﻿—and, what’s more, it’d only make things worse. If people find out you’re runnin’ after ’em they think you’re cheap, and then they won’t do as much for you as if you let ’em alone. I don’t believe it’s any use, and I couldn’t do it if it was.”
He sighed with resignation. “All right, mamma. That’s all.” Then, in a livelier tone, he said: “Ole Gurney took the bandages off my hand this morning. All healed up. Says I don’t need ’em any more.”

===Chinese===

“你不去试一试？”
“我不肯冒叫她把我撵出门去的险。不知怎么，我相信她就是这么对付西比尔的——照罗斯科有一回的话听来。不，我*不能*——再说，那只会把事情弄得更糟。人家要是看出你在追着他们跑，就当你下贱，往后给你办的反倒不如你放着不管的时候多。我不信这有什么用，就算有用我也做不来。”
他无可奈何地叹了口气。“好吧，妈妈。就说这些。”随后他换了活泼些的声调说：“老格尼今天早上把我手上的绷带拆了。全长好了。说我再用不着那玩意儿了。”

===Original===

“Why, that’s splendid, papa!” she cried, beaming. “I was afraid﻿—Let’s see.”
She came toward him, but he rose, still keeping his hand in his pocket. “Wait a minute,” he said, smiling. “Now it may give you just a teeny bit of a shock, but the fact is﻿—well, you remember that Sunday when Sibyl came over here and made all that fuss about nothin’﻿—it was the day after I got tired o’ that statue when Edith’s telegram came﻿—”
“Let me see your hand!” she cried.

===Chinese===

“哎呀，那可太好了，爸爸！”她满面喜色地叫起来。“我一直担心着——让我瞧瞧。”
她朝他走过去，他却站起身，手依旧插在口袋里。“等一等，”他笑着说。“这会儿也许要叫你小小吃一惊，可实情是——呃，你记得那个礼拜天吧，西比尔上这儿来无端大闹了一场——正是我对着那座像倒了胃口、伊迪丝电报来的第二天——”
“让我看看你的手！”她叫道。

===Original===

“Now wait!” he said, laughing and pushing her away with his left hand. “The truth is, mamma, that I kind o’ slipped out on you that morning, when you wasn’t lookin’, and went down to ole Gurney’s office﻿—he’d told me to, you see﻿—and, well, it doesn’t *amount* to anything.” And he held out, for her inspection, the mutilated hand. “You see, these days when it’s all dictatin’, anyhow, nobody’d mind just a couple o’﻿—”
He had to jump for her﻿—she went over backward. For the second time in her life Mrs. Sheridan fainted.

===Chinese===

“别急嘛！”他笑着，用左手把她推开。“实情是，妈妈，那天早上我趁你不留神，偷偷溜出去，下坡到老格尼的诊所去了——你瞧，是他吩咐我去的——呃，这实在*算不得*什么。”说着他把那只残手伸出来，给她验看。“你明白，如今反正全是口述，少那么区区一两根，没人会计较——”
他不得不一个箭步扑上去接她——她朝后直挺挺倒了下去。谢里丹太太有生以来第二回昏了过去。

## XXXII / 三十二

===Original===

It was a full hour later when he left her lying upon a couch in her own room, still lamenting intermittently, though he assured her with heat that the fuss she was making irked him far more than his physical loss. He permitted her to think that he meant to return directly to his office, but when he came out to the open air he told the chauffeur in attendance to await him in front of Mr. Vertrees’s house, whither he himself proceeded on foot.

Mr. Vertrees had taken the sale of half of his worthless stock as manna in the wilderness; it came from heaven﻿—by what agency he did not particularly question. The broker informed him that “parties were interested in getting hold of the stock,” and that later there might be a possible increase in the value of the large amount retained by his client. It might go “quite a ways up” within a year or so, he said, and he advised “sitting tight” with it. Mr. Vertrees went home and prayed.

He rose from his knees feeling that he was surely coming into his own again. It was more than a mere gasp of temporary relief with him, and his wife shared his optimism; but Mary would not let him buy back her piano, and as for furs﻿—spring was on the way, she said. But they paid the butcher, the baker, and the candlestick-maker, and hired a cook once more. It was this servitress who opened the door for Sheridan and presently assured him that Miss Vertrees would “be down.”

He was not the man to conceal admiration when he felt it, and he flushed and beamed as Mary made her appearance, almost upon the heels of the cook. She had a look of apprehension for the first fraction of a second, but it vanished at the sight of him, and its place was taken in her eyes by a soft brilliance, while color rushed in her cheeks.

===Chinese===

过了整整一个钟头，他才离开她——她躺在自己房里的软榻上，还在时断时续地哀哀切切，虽然他气冲冲地向她担保过：她这样大惊小怪，比他肉体上受的那点损失更叫他心烦。他由着她当真以为他要径直回办公室去；可一走到露天底下，他就吩咐伺候着的汽车夫把车停在弗特里斯先生家门前等候，自己则步行着往那里去了。

弗特里斯先生把卖掉他那半堆废股票的事，看作旷野里降下的吗哪（manna）；这是天上掉下来的——经由什么人之手，他没有特别去追问。经纪人告诉他说，“有当事方面有意收购这支股票”，还说往后他客户手里保留的那一大批，价值兴许还有上涨的可能。他说，一年半载之内它没准儿还能“往上蹿好大一截”，并劝他“捂住不动”。弗特里斯先生回到家去，做了祷告。

他从跪祷中站起来，觉得自己分明是要时来运转了。这在他已经不止是喘一口临时救急的气，他妻子也同他一样乐观；可是玛丽不许他赎回她的钢琴，至于皮衣嘛——春天快到了，她说。不过肉铺、面包坊，连烛台匠的账，他们都付清了，而且重新雇了一个厨娘。给谢里丹开门的正是这位厨娘，她随即向他担保，弗特里斯小姐这就“下来”。

他本不是那种心里起了钦慕还要藏掖的人；玛丽几乎紧跟着厨娘的脚后跟现了身，他登时红着脸，满面放光。头一霎之间她脸上掠过一丝惊惶，可一看见是他，那惊惶便无影无踪，她的眼睛里换上一种柔和的亮彩，两颊也涌起了红晕。

===Original===

“Don’t be surprised,” he said. “Truth is, in a way it’s sort of on business I looked in here. It’ll only take a minute, I expect.”

“I’m sorry,” said Mary. “I hoped you’d come because we’re neighbors.”

He chuckled. “Neighbors! Sometimes people don’t see so much o’ their neighbors as they used to. That is, I hear so﻿—lately.”

“You’ll stay long enough to sit down, won’t you?”

“I guess I could manage that much.” And they sat down, facing each other and not far apart.

===Chinese===

“你别诧异，”他说。“实话说，我上这儿来，多少也算办点事。我想，一分钟就完。”

“真可惜，”玛丽说。“我原指望您来，是因为我们是邻居。”

他呵呵一笑。“邻居！如今人见邻居，可不见得像从前见得那么勤了。这话嘛——我是听来的。近来。”

“您总肯坐下待一会儿，是不是？”

“我想这一条我还对付得了。”于是两人坐下来，面对面，离得不远。

===Original===

“Of course, it couldn’t be called business, exactly,” he said, more gravely. “Not at all, I expect. But there’s something o’ yours it seemed to me I ought to give you, and I just thought it was better to bring it myself and explain how I happened to have it. It’s this﻿—this letter you wrote my boy.” He extended the letter to her solemnly, in his left hand, and she took it gently from him. “It was in his mail, after he was hurt. You knew he never got it, I expect.”

“Yes,” she said, in a low voice.

He sighed. “I’m glad he didn’t. Not,” he added, quickly﻿—“not but what you did just right to send it. You did. You couldn’t acted any other way when it came right down *to* it. There ain’t any blame comin’ to you﻿—you were aboveboard all through.”

Mary said, “Thank you,” almost in a whisper, and with her head bowed low.

“You’ll have to excuse me for readin’ it. I had to take charge of all his mail and everything; I didn’t know the handwritin’, and I read it all﻿—once I got started.”

===Chinese===

“当然，要说办事，其实也算不上，”他说，语气郑重了些。“压根儿算不上，我琢磨。只是有一样东西是你的，我觉得应当还给你，我又寻思，不如自个儿送来，顺便讲讲这东西怎么就到了我手里。就是这个——你写给我那孩子的信。”他把信庄庄重重地用左手递过去，她轻轻地从他手里接了。“信夹在他的邮件里，他受了伤以后。想来你也知道，他始终没收到。”

“是的，”她低低地说。

他叹了口气。“他没收到，我心里倒欢喜。倒不是，”他赶忙找补一句，“倒不是说你把信寄出去寄得不合适。你做得对。真*到*了那一步，你不可能有旁的做法。这事儿一点儿怪不着你——你自始至终光明磊落。”

玛丽说了声“谢谢”，声音低得几乎成了耳语，头深深地垂着。

“我把它读了，你可得担待。他的邮件样样都得我来管；那笔迹我不认得，一开了头，我就全念完了。”

===Original===

“I’m glad you did.”

“Well”﻿—he leaned forward as if to rise﻿—“I guess that’s about all. I just thought you ought to have it.”

“Thank you for bringing it.”

He looked at her hopefully, as if he thought and wished that she might have something more to say. But she seemed not to be aware of this glance, and sat with her eyes fixed sorrowfully upon the floor.

“Well, I expect I better be gettin’ back to the office,” he said, rising desperately. “I told﻿—I told my partner I’d be back at two o’clock, and I guess he’ll think I’m a poor business man if he catches me behind time. I got to walk the chalk a mighty straight line these days﻿—with *that* fellow keepin’ tabs on me!”

===Chinese===

“您读了，我很高兴。”

“那么”——他探身向前，像要起身——“我想也就是这些了。我只觉得，这信理应归你。”

“谢谢您送来。”

他满怀指望地望着她，仿佛他既想着又盼着她兴许还有话要讲。可她似乎并没有觉察这一眼，只坐在那儿，一双眼睛哀哀地盯着地板。

“这么着，我想我顶好回办公室去了，”他说着，没奈何似的站起身。“我跟——我跟我的合伙人说了，两点钟回去；叫他逮着我误了点，他准得说我是个不中用的买卖人。这些日子我可得照着粉笔线走，走得端端正正——有*那*位成天盯我的梢呢！”

===Original===

Mary rose with him. “I’ve always heard *you* were the hard driver.”

He guffawed derisively. “Me? I’m nothin’ to that partner o’ mine. You couldn’t guess to save your life how he keeps after me to hold up my end o’ the job. I shouldn’t be surprised he’d give me the grand bounce some day, and run the whole circus by himself. You know how he is﻿—once he goes *at* a thing!”

“No,” she smiled. “I didn’t know you had a partner. I’d always heard﻿—”

He laughed, looking away from her. “It’s just my way o’ speakin’ o’ that boy o’ mine, Bibbs.”

He stood then, expectant, staring out into the hall with an air of careless geniality. He felt that she certainly must at least say, “How *is* Bibbs?” but she said nothing at all, though he waited until the silence became embarrassing.

===Chinese===

玛丽随他站起身。“我一直听说，硬逼着人干的那个，是*您*。”

他嘲讽地放声大笑。“我？跟那位合伙人比，我算个啥。你就是拿性命打赌也猜不着，他怎么一个劲儿催我，非要我把自个儿那份活儿顶起来。保不齐哪天他一脚把我踢出门去，整个马戏班子他独个儿张罗起来。你是知道他那脾气的——一旦朝一件事*扑*上去！”

“不知道，”她微笑道。“我原不知道您还有个合伙人。我一直听说——”

他笑着，眼睛避开了她。“那不过是我说起我那个孩子比布斯时的说法罢了。”

于是他站在那儿，满心期待，眼睛望着门厅外面，摆出一副随随便便的和气神情。他觉着，她至少总得问一句，“比布斯*好*么？”可她竟一声不响，他一直等，等到那份沉默叫人难堪起来。

===Original===

“Well, I guess I better be gettin’ down there,” he said, at last. “He might worry.”

“Goodbye﻿—and thank you,” said Mary.

“For what?”

“For the letter.”

“Oh,” he said, blankly. “You’re welcome. Goodbye.”

===Chinese===

“这么着，我想我顶好还是下那边去，”他末了说。“他兴许要不放心呢。”

“再会——还有，谢谢您，”玛丽说。

“谢什么？”

“谢那封信。”

“哦，”他茫然地说。“不客气。再会。”

===Original===

Mary put out her hand. “Goodbye.”

“You’ll have to excuse my left hand,” he said. “I had a little accident to the other one.”

She gave a pitying cry as she saw. “Oh, poor Mr. Sheridan!”

“Nothin’ at all! Dictate everything nowadays, anyhow.” He laughed jovially. “Did anybody tell you how it happened?”

“I heard you hurt your hand, but no﻿—not just how.”

===Chinese===

玛丽伸出手去。“再会。”

“只得请您担待我用的是左手，”他说。“那一只出了点儿小事故。”

她一看见，发出一声怜惜的惊叫。“哎呀，可怜的谢里丹先生！”

“不碍事！如今样样都是口述，横竖用不着手。”他乐呵呵地笑了。“事情是怎么出的，有人讲给你听了么？”

“听说您伤了手，可是没有——没听说究竟怎么伤的。”

===Original===

“It was this way,” he began, and both, as if unconsciously, sat down again. “You may not know it, but I used to worry a good deal about the youngest o’ my boys﻿—the one that used to come to see you sometimes, after Jim﻿—that is, I mean Bibbs. He’s the one I spoke of as my partner; and the truth is that’s what it’s just about goin’ to amount to, one o’ these days﻿—if his health holds out. Well, you remember, I expect, I had him on a machine over at a plant o’ mine; and sometimes I’d kind o’ sneak in there and see how he was gettin’ along. Take a doctor with me sometimes, because Bibbs never *was* so robust, you might say. Ole Doc Gurney﻿—I guess maybe you know him? Tall, thin man; acts sleepy﻿—”

“Yes.”

“Well, one day I an’ ole Doc Gurney, we were in there, and I undertook to show Bibbs how to run his machine. He told me to look out, but I wouldn’t listen, and I didn’t look out﻿—and that’s how I got my hand hurt, tryin’ to show Bibbs how to do something he knew how to do and I didn’t. Made me so mad I just wouldn’t even admit to myself it *was* hurt﻿—and so, by and by, ole Doc Gurney had to take kind o’ radical measures with me. He’s a right good doctor, too. Don’t you think so, Miss Vertrees?”

“Yes.”

“Yes, he is so!” Sheridan now had the air of a rambling talker and gossip with all day on his hands. “Take him on Bibbs’s case. I was talkin’ about Bibbs’s case with him this morning. Well, you’d laugh to hear the way ole Gurney talks about *that*! ’Course he *is* just as much a friend as he is doctor﻿—and he takes as much interest in Bibbs as if he was in the family. He says Bibbs isn’t anyways bad off *yet*; and he thinks he could stand the pace and get fat on it if﻿—well, this is what’d made *you* laugh if you’d been there, Miss Vertrees﻿—honest it would!” He paused to chuckle, and stole a glance at her. She was gazing straight before her at the wall; her lips were parted, and﻿—visibly﻿—she was breathing heavily and quickly. He feared that she was growing furiously angry; but he had led to what he wanted to say, and he went on, determined now to say it all. He leaned forward and altered his voice to one of confidential friendliness, though in it he still maintained a tone which indicated that ole Doc Gurney’s opinion was only a joke he shared with her. “Yes, sir, you certainly would ’a’ laughed! Why, that ole man thinks *you* got something to do with it. You’ll have to blame it on him, young lady, if it makes you feel like startin’ out to whip somebody! He’s actually got *this* theory: he says Bibbs got to gettin’ better while he worked over there at the shop because you kept him cheered up and feelin’ good. And he says if you could manage to just stand him hangin’ around a little﻿—maybe not much, but just *some*times﻿—again, he believed it’d do Bibbs a mighty lot o’ good. ’Course, that’s only what the doctor said. Me, I don’t know anything about that; but I can say this much﻿—I never saw any such a *mental* improvement in anybody in my life as I have lately in Bibbs. I expect you’d find him a good deal more entertaining than what he used to be﻿—and I know it’s a kind of embarrassing thing to suggest after the way he piled in over here that day to ask you to stand up before the preacher with him, but accordin’ to ole Doc *Gurney*, he’s got you on his brain so bad﻿—”

===Chinese===

“是这么回事，”他开了头，两个人都像是不知不觉地重新坐了下来。“你兴许还不知道，我那顶小的儿子——吉姆过后，有时候来看你的那一个——我是说，我指的是比布斯——从前着实叫我操了不少心。我说过的那个合伙人，就是他；说真的，要不了几天，这差不多就要成了实事——只要他的身子骨撑得住。哦，你大约还记得，我曾把他安在我一座厂里，开一台机器；有时候我会溜达进去，看看他干得怎么样。有时还带个医生同去，因为比布斯那个身子板，*向来*就不算壮实，可以这么说。老格尼——你兴许认得他吧？高高瘦瘦的一个人，老是一副没睡醒的样子——”

“是的。”

“哦，有一天，我跟老格尼两个正在那儿，我逞能，要教比布斯怎么开他那台机器。他叫我当心，我不肯听，结果也没当心——我的手就这么伤的：为着要教比布斯一件他会做、我不会做的事。这叫我气得呀，连自己都不肯承认它*是*伤了——末了，老格尼只好对我下了点儿狠招。他可是个呱呱叫的好医生。你说是不是，弗特里斯小姐？”

“是的。”

“可不是，他就是好！”谢里丹这会儿已经换上一副东拉西扯、闲话连篇的神气，仿佛手里攥着一整天闲工夫。“单说比布斯这个病例，就够讲一气。今儿早上我还跟他谈比布斯的病例来着。哦，你要是听见老格尼讲起*那个*，准得笑！他嘛，朋友的身份，*确*是同医生的身份一样多——他关心比布斯，就跟比布斯是他自家人似的。他说比布斯*眼下*还谈不上怎么糟；他还觉得，这种步子比布斯扛得住，还能靠它养得肥肥的，只要——哦，就是底下这一句，你要是在场，弗特里斯小姐，*你*准得笑——真格的，保管你笑！”他停住，呵呵一笑，偷眼看她。她直勾勾地望着面前的墙；嘴唇微微张着，而且——看得分明——她的呼吸又沉又急。他怕她是气得火冒上来了；可他话头已经引到要说的正题上，便接着往下讲，这回横下心来要一气讲完。他把身子凑前，嗓音换成一种推心置腹的和软，只是话音里仍旧拿捏着一点腔调，表明老格尼的高见不过是爷儿俩同她分享的一个笑话。“是的，先生，你要是在场，保管早就笑了！你瞧，那老头儿认定，这上头有*你*的一份功劳。姑娘家，倘或你气得想出去抽谁一顿，这笔账你可只有记到他头上！他当真有*这么一条*道理：他说，比布斯在厂里做事那阵子见好，是因为你叫他心里痛快，觉着舒坦。他还说，倘或你能想法子，再容他到跟前转悠转悠——用不着多，隔三差五*偶*尔那么几回就成——他信这个能给比布斯带来天大的好处。当然，这只是医生说的。我呢，这个我可一窍不通；不过有一点我可以讲——我活了这辈子，从没见过谁*精神*上的起色有比布斯近来这么大。我琢磨，你会发现他比从前有味儿得多——我也知道，那天他一头闯到这儿来，求你跟他并排站到牧师跟前，闹了那么一场，如今再提这话，是有点儿叫人难为情；可是照老格*尼*的说法，他满脑子装的都是你，装得可厉害——”

===Original===

Mary jumped. “Mr. Sheridan!” she exclaimed.

He sighed profoundly. “There! I noticed you were gettin’ mad. I didn’t﻿—”

“No, no, no!” she cried. “But I don’t understand﻿—and I think you don’t. What is it you want me to do?”

He sighed again, but this time with relief. “Well, well!” he said. “You’re right. It’ll be easier to talk plain. I ought to known I could with you, all the time. I just hoped you’d let that boy come and see you sometimes, once more. Could you?”

“You don’t understand.” She clasped her hands together in a sorrowful gesture. “Yes, we must talk plain. Bibbs heard that I’d tried to make your oldest son care for me because I was poor, and so Bibbs came and asked me to marry him﻿—because he was sorry for me. And I *can’t* see him any more,” she cried in distress. “I *can’t*!”

===Chinese===

玛丽一惊。“谢里丹先生！”她失声叫道。

他深深地叹了口气。“瞧瞧！我就看出来你要恼了。我没有——”

“不，不，不！”她叫道。“可是我不明白——而且我想，您自己也不明白。您究竟要我做什么？”

他又叹了口气，可这回是松了口气。“好啦，好啦！”他说。“你说得对，直截了当地讲反倒容易。我早该知道，跟你是向来能直说的。我只盼你肯让那孩子再来瞧瞧你，隔三差五再来一回。你肯么？”

“您不明白。”她两手相握，比了个哀苦的姿势。“好，我们是非直说不可了。比布斯听人说，我因为穷，存心要叫您的大儿子爱上我；于是比布斯来向我求婚——那是因为他可怜我。所以我*不能*再见他了，”她苦痛地喊道。“我*不能*！”

===Original===

Sheridan cleared his throat uncomfortably. “You mean because he thought that about you?”

“No, no! What he thought was *true*!”

“Well﻿—you mean he was so much in﻿—you mean he thought so much of you﻿—” The words were inconceivably awkward upon Sheridan’s tongue; he seemed to be in doubt even about pronouncing them, but after a ghastly pause he bravely repeated them. “You mean he thought so much of you that you just couldn’t stand him around?”

“*No!* He was sorry for me. He cared for me; he was fond of me; and he’d respected me﻿—too much! In the finest way he loved me, if you like, and he’d have done anything on earth for me, as I would for him, and as he knew I would. It was beautiful, Mr. Sheridan,” she said. “But the cheap, bad things one has done seem always to come back﻿—they wait, and pull you down when you’re happiest. Bibbs found me out, you see; and he wasn’t ‘in love’ with me at all.”

“He wasn’t? Well, it seems to me he gave up everything he wanted to do﻿—it was fool stuff, but he certainly wanted it mighty bad﻿—he just threw it away and walked right up and took the job he swore he never would﻿—just for you. And it looks to me as if a man that’d do that must think quite a heap o’ the girl he does it for! You say it was only because he was sorry, but let me tell you there’s only *one* girl he could feel *that* sorry for! Yes, sir!”

===Chinese===

谢里丹不自在地清了清嗓子。“你是说，因为他把你想成了那样？”

“不，不！他想的那样是*真的*！”

“哦——你是说他恋得那样深——你是说他那样地看重你——”这几句话在谢里丹舌头上笨拙得不可思议；他似乎连出口都觉得迟疑，可是挨过一阵叫人心惊的停顿之后，他硬着头皮又说了一遍。“你是说，他那样地看重你，以致你简直容不得他在跟前？”

“*不！*他是可怜我。他心疼我；他喜欢我；他还敬重我——敬重得过了头！随你怎么说，他是在顶堂皇的路上爱我的；他肯为我在世上做任何事，我也会为他做任何事，他也知道我会。这本是极美的，谢里丹先生，”她说。“可是一个人做下的那些下贱的、坏的事，好像总会回来——它们等着，等你最快活的时候把你拽倒。比布斯把我的底细看穿了，你明白么；他压根儿没‘爱’上我。”

“他不曾？哦，照我看，他把自己想做的事全撂下了——那是桩傻里傻气的营生，可他着实想要得要命——他一把扔了个干净，径直走上前去，接下他发过誓死也不接的差事——单是为了你。照我看，一个男人肯这样做，他对那个姑娘的心思总归沉得很！你说这只是因为他可怜你，可我告诉你，天底下只有*一个*姑娘，他能可怜到*那个*地步！是的，先生！”

===Original===

“No, no,” she said. “Bibbs isn’t like other men﻿—he would do anything for anybody.”

Sheridan grinned. “Perhaps not so much as you think, nowadays,” he said. “For instance, I got kind of a suspicion he doesn’t believe in sentiment in business. But that’s neither here nor there. What he wanted was, just plain and simple, for you to marry him. Well, I was afraid his thinkin’ so much *of* you had kind o’ sickened you of him﻿—the way it does sometimes. But from the way you talk, I understand that ain’t the trouble.” He coughed, and his voice trembled a little. “Now here, Miss Vertrees, I don’t have to tell you﻿—because you see things easy﻿—I know I got no business comin’ to you like this, but I had to make Bibbs go my way instead of his own﻿—I had to do it for the sake o’ my business and on his own account, too﻿—and I expect you got some idea how it hurt him to give up. Well, he’s made good. He didn’t come in halfhearted or mean; he came in﻿—all the way! But there isn’t anything in it to him; you can see he’s just shut his teeth on it and goin’ ahead with dust in his mouth. You see, one way of lookin’ at it, he’s got nothin’ to work *for*. And it seems to me like it cost him your friendship, and I believe﻿—honest﻿—that’s what hurt him the worst. Now you said we’d talk plain. Why can’t you let him come back?”

She covered her face desperately with her hands. “I can’t!”

He rose, defeated, and looking it.

“Well, I mustn’t press you,” he said, gently.

===Chinese===

“不，不，”她说。“比布斯跟旁的男子不一样——他会为任何人做任何事。”

谢里丹咧嘴一笑。“如今兴许不见得有你想的那么多，”他说。“比如说，我多少有点疑心，他在买卖上不信什么情分。不过这也不相干。他要的原是顶简单顶直白的一件事：要你嫁给他。哦，我先前还怕，他这样*看重*你，倒叫你见了他就倒胃口——有时候是有这么个情形的。可听你这口气，我明白症结不在这儿。”他咳嗽了一声，嗓音有点儿发颤。“那么，弗特里斯小姐，这不用我说你也明白——你看事情一看就透——我知道，我这样上门来是没有道理的；可我不得不叫比布斯依我的道儿走，别依他自己的道儿——为我买卖的缘故我不得不这样，为他自个儿的缘故也是这样——我想他舍弃的时候有多疼，你也略知一二。哦，他到底成了事。他进来的时候，不是三心二意，也不是小里小气；他进来，就进到了底！可这桩事在他已一无所有；你看得出来，他只是咬紧牙关咬住它，满嘴尘土还往前赶。你想想，从一面上看，他做活已经没有个*为*头了。照我看，这一来他搭上了你的交情，我信——真心信——伤他伤得最狠的正是这个。你方才说，我们要直截了当地讲。你怎么就不能让他回来呢？”

她没奈何地用两手掩住脸。“我不能！”

他站起身来，吃了败仗，一副吃了败仗的模样。

“好，我不该逼你，”他温声说。

===Original===

At that she cried out, and dropped her hands and let him see her face. “Ah! He was only sorry for me!”

He gazed at her intently. Mary was proud, but she had a fatal honesty, and it confessed the truth of her now; she was helpless. It was so clear that even Sheridan, marveling and amazed, was able to see it. Then a change came over him; gloom fell from him, and he grew radiant.

“Don’t! Don’t!” she cried. “You mustn’t﻿—”

“I won’t tell him,” said Sheridan, from the doorway. “I won’t tell anybody anything!”

===Chinese===

一听这话她叫了出来，放下两手，让他看见她的脸。“啊！他只是可怜我！”

他定定地凝望她。玛丽是骄傲的，可她有一种要命的诚实，此刻这诚实把她心底的真供了出来；她是身不由己的了。这是再分明不过的，连谢里丹——又惊又诧——也看了出来。接着他身上起了个变化；阴翳从他脸上褪落，他变得满面焕然生光。

“别说！别说！”她叫道。“您可千万不能——”

“我不会对他讲的，”谢里丹在门口说。“我什么也不会对任何人讲！”

## XXXIII / 三十三

===Original===
There was a heavy town-fog that afternoon, a smoke-mist, densest in the sanctuary of the temple. The people went about in it, busy and dirty, thickening their outside and inside linings of coal-tar, asphalt, sulphurous acid, oil of vitriol, and the other familiar things the men liked to breathe and to have upon their skins and garments and upon their wives and babies and sweethearts. The growth of the city was visible in the smoke and the noise and the rush. There was more smoke than there had been this day of February a year earlier; there was more noise; and the crowds were thicker﻿—yet quicker in spite of that. The traffic policeman had a hard time, for the people were independent﻿—they retained some habits of the old market-town period, and would cross the street anywhere and anyhow, which not only got them killed more frequently than if they clung to the legal crossings, but kept the motormen, the chauffeurs, and the truck-drivers in a stew of profane nervousness. So the traffic policemen led harried lives; they themselves were killed, of course, with a certain periodicity, but their main trouble was that they could not make the citizens realize that it was actually and mortally perilous to go about their city. It was strange, for there were probably no citizens of any length of residence who had not personally known either someone who had been killed or injured in an accident, or someone who had accidentally killed or injured others. And yet, perhaps it was not strange, seeing the sharp preoccupation of the faces﻿—the people had something on their minds; they could not stop to bother about dirt and danger.

Mary Vertrees was not often downtown; she had never seen an accident until this afternoon. She had come upon errands for her mother connected with a timorous refurbishment; and as she did these, in and out of the department stores, she had an insistent consciousness of the Sheridan Building. From the street, anywhere, it was almost always in sight, like some monstrous geometrical shadow, murk-colored and rising limitlessly into the swimming heights of the smoke-mist. It was gaunt and grimy and repellent; it had nothing but strength and size﻿—but in that consciousness of Mary’s the great structure may have partaken of beauty. Sheridan had made some of the things he said emphatic enough to remain with her. She went over and over them﻿—and they began to seem true: “Only *one* girl he could feel *that* sorry for!” “Gurney says he’s got you on his brain so bad﻿—” The man’s clumsy talk began to sing in her heart. The song was begun there when she saw the accident.

She was directly opposite the Sheridan Building then, waiting for the traffic to thin before she crossed, though other people were risking the passage, darting and halting and dodging parlously. Two men came from the crowd behind her, talking earnestly, and started across. Both wore black; one was tall and broad and thick, and the other was taller, but noticeably slender. And Mary caught her breath, for they were Bibbs and his father. They did not see her, and she caught a phrase in Bibbs’s mellow voice, which had taken a crisper ring: “Sixty-eight thousand dollars? Not sixty-eight thousand buttons!” It startled her queerly, and as there was a glimpse of his profile she saw for the first time a resemblance to his father.

===Chinese===
那天下午，城里罩着一层浓重的市雾，一片烟霭，在圣殿的至圣所里雾得最稠。人们在其间奔走，忙碌而肮脏，把自己里里外外的衬里越敷越厚：煤焦油、沥青、亚硫酸、矾油，还有旁的那些熟东西——男人们顶喜欢呼吸它们，喜欢叫它们沾上自己的皮肤和衣裳，沾上老婆、娃娃和心上人。这座城的生长，在烟雾里、在闹声里、在急流里都看得见：比一年前的这个二月日子，烟更浓了，闹声更响了，人也更稠了——然而偏偏还更匆忙。街口警察的日子不好过，因为市民们自作主张——他们还带着旧日集镇时代的几分老习惯，想从哪儿过街就从哪儿过街，爱怎么过就怎么过；这么一来，不但他们送命的次数比守着法定横道过街要多，还把电车司机、汽车司机、卡车司机个个熬得神经绷紧、骂骂咧咧。于是街口警察过着备受煎熬的日子；他们自己当然也按着一定的周期挨车撞死，可他们顶大的苦处，是没法叫市民们明白：在这座城里走动，实在是性命交关的危险。说来也怪，凡是住得久些的市民，恐怕没有谁不曾亲身认识一个在车祸里送命或受伤的人，或是无意间撞死撞伤了旁人的人。可话说回来，也许并不怪，只消看看一张张脸那种急切的专注——人们心里都压着事儿，顾不上停下来为肮脏和危险费神。

玛丽·弗特里斯不大下城来；直到今天下午为止，她从没见过一场横祸。她是替母亲出来办事的，办的是几桩同一次战战兢兢的翻修相干的差事；她进进出出于各家百货公司，一路办着这些事，谢里丹大厦却一味地闯上她的心头，挥之不去。不论从哪条街上望，它几乎总是看得见，像一具庞大得出奇的几何形黑影，颜色昏黑，没个尽头地升进烟霭荡漾的高处。它嶙峋、污黑、拒人千里；它有的只是力气和个头儿——可在玛丽这份意识里，这座庞然大物或许也分沾了几分美。谢里丹从前说过的有些话，说得够重，留在她心上没走。她把它们翻来覆去地想——它们开始显得像是真的：“他能疼到*那个*地步的姑娘，只有*一个*！”“格尼说了，他满脑子搁的都是你，搁得可不轻——”这个人笨嘴拙舌的谈吐，开始在她心里唱起来了。就在她望见那场横祸的时候，那支歌已经在那里开了头。

这时候她正站在谢里丹大厦的正对面，等着车马稀一稀再过街，虽则旁的人已经在冒险抢渡，蹿一蹿、停一停、东躲西闪，险象环生。有两个男人从她背后的人堆里出来，一面郑重其事地谈着，一面起步过街。两人都穿黑衣裳；一个高大、宽阔、厚实，另一个更高些，却分明清瘦。玛丽屏住了气：那是比布斯和他的父亲。他们没有瞧见她，她听见比布斯圆润的嗓音里飘出一句来，那嗓音添了一层更清脆的响劲儿：“六万八千美元？六万八千颗纽扣也不给！”这话古怪地吓了她一跳，而他侧脸一闪，她头一回看出他像他的父亲。

===Original===
She watched them. In the middle of the street Bibbs had to step ahead of his father, and the two were separated. But the reckless passing of a truck, beyond the second line of rails, frightened a group of country women who were in course of passage; they were just in front of Bibbs, and shoved backward upon him violently. To extricate himself from them he stepped back, directly in front of a moving trolley-car﻿—no place for absentmindedness, but Bibbs was still absorbed in thoughts concerned with what he had been saying to his father. There were shrieks and yells; Bibbs looked the wrong way﻿—and then Mary saw the heavy figure of Sheridan plunge straight forward in front of the car. With absolute disregard of his own life, he hurled himself at Bibbs like a football-player shunting off an opponent, and to Mary it seemed that they both went down together. But that was all she could see﻿—automobiles, trucks, and wagons closed in between. She made out that the trolley-car stopped jerkily, and she saw a policeman breaking his way through the instantly condensing crowd, while the traffic came to a standstill, and people stood up in automobiles or climbed upon the hubs and tires of wheels, not to miss a chance of seeing anything horrible.

Mary tried to get through; it was impossible. Other policemen came to help the first, and in a minute or two the traffic was in motion again. The crowd became pliant, dispersing﻿—there was no figure upon the ground, and no ambulance came. But one of the policemen was detained by the clinging and beseeching of a gloved hand.

“What *is* the matter, lady?”

===Chinese===
她望着他们。到了街心，比布斯不得不抢到父亲前头去，两个人便分开了。可就在第二条铁轨的那一边，一辆卡车肆无忌惮地疾驶而过，惊着了一伙正在过街的乡下女人；她们恰好在比布斯跟前，猛地朝后挤到他身上。为了从她们当中脱身，他退后一步，恰恰退到一辆正在开动的电车前面——这种地方顶容不得心不在焉，可比布斯的心思还沉在他方才同父亲说的那些话里。尖叫和喊声炸成一片；比布斯朝错的方向望去——跟着，玛丽就看见谢里丹那魁伟的身躯直直地扑到电车前头去。他全然不顾自己的性命，像橄榄球员拨开对手那样朝比布斯撞了过去；在玛丽看来，他们两个是一齐倒下去的。可她能望见的也只有这些——汽车、卡车、货车合拢来，把中间的一切都遮断了。她看得出电车猛地刹住了，又看见一名警察从霎时聚拢来的人堆里挤开一条路；车马全都停摆了，人们从汽车里站起身，或是爬上车轮毂和轮胎，生怕错过一点看的机会，不管那东西有多么可怕。

玛丽想挤过去；挤不过去。别的警察赶来帮头一个的忙，一两分钟之后，车马又开动了。人群服帖下来，四散而去——地上没有躺着人影，也没有救护车开来。可有那么一个警察，让一只戴着手套的手缠住不放、苦苦哀告，脱不开身。

“太太，到底*是*出了什么事？”

===Original===
“Where are they?” Mary cried.

“Who? Ole man Sheridan? I reckon *he* wasn’t much hurt!”

“His *son*﻿—”

“Was that who the other one was? I seen him knock him﻿—oh, he’s not bad off, I guess, lady. The ole man got him out of the way all right. The fender shoved the ole man around some, but I reckon he only got shook up. They both went on in the Sheridan Building without any help. Excuse me, lady.”

===Chinese===
“他们在哪儿？”玛丽嚷道。

“谁？谢里丹老爷子？我看*他*伤不着哪儿！”

“他的*儿子*——”

“哦，另一个原来是他儿子？我瞅见他把他撞开的——哦，我看他没什么大碍，太太。老爷子把他拨拉到一边，稳稳当当。挡板把老爷子蹭得打了个转儿，不过我看也就是震懵了一阵。他们爷儿俩没人搀扶，自己进了谢里丹大厦。失陪了，太太。”

===Original===
Sheridan and Bibbs, in fact, were at that moment in the elevator, ascending. “Whisk-broom up in the office,” Sheridan was saying. “You got to look out on those corners nowadays, I tell you. I don’t know I got any call to blow, though﻿—because I tried to cross after you did. That’s how I happened to run into you. Well, you want to remember to look out after this. We were talkin’ about Murtrie’s askin’ sixty-eight thousand flat for that ninety-nine-year lease. It’s his lookout if he’d rather take it that way, and I don’t know but﻿—”

“No,” said Bibbs, emphatically, as the elevator stopped; “he won’t get it. Not from us, he won’t, and I’ll show you why. I can convince you in five minutes.” He followed his father into the office anteroom﻿—and convinced him. Then, having been diligently brushed by a youth of color, Bibbs went into his own room and closed the door.

He was more shaken than he had allowed his father to perceive, and his side was sore where Sheridan had struck him. He desired to be alone; he wanted to rub himself and, for once, to do some useless thinking again. He knew that his father had not “happened” to run into him; he knew that Sheridan had instantly﻿—and instinctively﻿—proved that he held his own life of no account whatever compared to that of his son and heir. Bibbs had been unable to speak of that, or to seem to know it; for Sheridan, just as instinctively, had swept the matter aside﻿—as of no importance, since all was well﻿—reverting immediately to business.

Bibbs began to think intently of his father. He perceived, as he had never perceived before, the shadowing of something enormous and indomitable﻿—and lawless; not to be daunted by the will of nature’s very self; laughing at the lightning and at wounds and mutilation; conquering, irresistible﻿—and blindly noble. For the first time in his life Bibbs began to understand the meaning of being truly this man’s son.

===Chinese===
其实这个时候，谢里丹和比布斯正在电梯里，一路往上升。“掸衣刷在楼上办公室里，”谢里丹说个不停。“如今在这些路口上你可得留神，我告诉你。不过呢，我也没有什么好自吹的——因为我是跟着你后头才要过街的。这才撞上了你。得，往后你可要记住留神。咱们方才正说着默特里那张九十九年租约，他一口价张口就要六万八。他乐意按这个价办，那是他自家的事，可我也说不好——”

“不成，”电梯停住的时候，比布斯斩钉截铁地说；“他拿不着。从咱们手里，他拿不着，我把缘故讲给你听。五分钟我就能叫你心服。”他跟着父亲走进办公室的前厅——当场就把他说服了。随后，叫一个有色人种的小伙子殷勤地掸刷了一通，比布斯走进自己那间屋子，把门关上了。

他心里翻腾得厉害，比他让父亲看出来的厉害得多；被谢里丹撞过的那半边身子还在作痛。他想独自待一会儿；想揉一揉自己碰疼的地方，还想——这回破个例——再做一回无用的思索。他知道父亲并不是“碰巧”撞上他的；他知道谢里丹当场——出于本能地——证明了一件事：同他儿子兼继承人的一条命相比，他把自己的性命看得一钱不值。这句话比布斯说不出口，也不能露出知道的样子；因为谢里丹同样出于本能，一挥手就把这桩事扫到了一旁——既然人平安，便不值一提——随即转回了买卖。

比布斯开始凝神想他的父亲。他看出某种庞大而不屈的东西投下的暗影——还带着一股目无法纪的气性；这是他从来不曾看清过的：连大自然自身的意志也吓它不退；它讥笑闪电，讥笑创伤与残毁；它一路征服，不可抗拒——而且盲目地高贵。平生头一遭，比布斯开始懂得，真正做这个人的儿子，究竟是怎么回事。

===Original===
He would be the more truly his son henceforth, though, as Sheridan said, Bibbs had not come downtown with him meanly or half-heartedly. He had given his word because he had wanted the money, simply, for Mary Vertrees in her need. And he shivered with horror of himself, thinking how he had gone to her to offer it, asking her to marry him﻿—with his head on his breast in shameful fear that she would accept him! He had not known her; the knowing had lost her to him, and this had been his real awakening; for he knew now how deep had been that slumber wherein he dreamily celebrated the superiority of “friendship”! The sleepwalker had wakened to bitter knowledge of love and life, finding himself a failure in both. He had made a burnt offering of his dreams, and the sacrifice had been an unforgivable hurt to Mary. All that was left for him was the work he had not chosen, but at least he would not fail in that, though it was indeed no more than dust in his mouth. If there had been anything to work for﻿—

He went to the window, raised it, and let in the uproar of the streets below. He looked down at the blurred, hurrying swarms and he looked across, over the roofs with their panting jets of vapor, into the vast, foggy heart of the smoke. Dizzy traceries of steel were rising dimly against it, chattering with steel on steel, and screeching in steam, while tiny figures of men walked on threads in the dull sky. Buildings would overtop the Sheridan. Bigness was being served.

But what for? The old question came to Bibbs with a new despair. Here, where his eyes fell, had once been green fields and running brooks, and how had the kind earth been despoiled and disfigured! The pioneers had begun the work, but in their old age their orators had said for them that they had toiled and risked and sacrificed that their posterity might live in peace and wisdom, enjoying the fruits of the earth. Well, their posterity was here﻿—and there was only turmoil. Where was the promised land? It had been promised by the soldiers of all the wars; it had been promised to this generation by the pioneers; but here was the very posterity to whom it had been promised, toiling and risking and sacrificing in turn﻿—for what?

The harsh roar of the city came in through the open window, continuously beating upon Bibbs’s ear until he began to distinguish a pulsation in it﻿—a broken and irregular cadence. It seemed to him that it was like a titanic voice, discordant, hoarse, rustily metallic﻿—the voice of the god, Bigness. And the voice summoned Bibbs as it summoned all its servants.

===Chinese===
不过从此往后，他要做得更像他的儿子——虽说正如谢里丹所说，比布斯跟他一道下城来，并不是敷衍，也不是三心二意。他之所以许下诺言，就是因为他要那笔钱，简单得很，为了危难之中的玛丽·弗特里斯。想到自己当时怎样走到她跟前去奉献那笔钱，还求她嫁给自己——脑袋耷拉在胸口，羞惭地害怕她当真会应承！——他对自己惊骇得打起寒噤来。他从前不了解她；一了解，却把她失掉了；这一下才真正是他醒过来的一刻；因为他如今才明白，在那场悠悠的酣睡里，他昏头昏脑地歌颂“友谊”的优越，睡得有多么沉。梦游的人醒了，苦涩地懂得了爱与生活，发觉自己在这两样上头都是失败者。他把自己的梦当燔祭献了上去，而这祭品给玛丽造成了不可饶恕的伤痛。留给他的，只剩下这份他并非自己选中的工作；可至少在这上头他不会再失败，虽说它在他嘴里实在不过是一把尘土。倘若还有什么东西值得为之操劳——

他走到窗前，把窗推上去，放楼下街市的喧嚣进来。他俯望下面那些模模糊糊、滚滚匆匆的人潮，又放眼望过去，越过一片片屋顶——屋顶上喷着一股股喘息似的白汽——望进烟雾那浩大、雾蒙蒙的心脏。叫人头晕的钢桁架影影绰绰地正朝它升上去，钢铁磕着钢铁，铿铿作响，蒸汽吱吱尖叫，而小小的人影在昏沉的天空里走着一根根细线。会有大楼高过谢里丹大厦的。庞大正受着侍奉。

可这是为了什么？那个老问题又来找比布斯，这一回带着新的绝望。他目光落下的这个地方，从前是绿野，是奔流的溪涧；这仁厚的大地遭到了怎样的劫夺、怎样的毁容啊！拓荒的人开了这个头；可到了他们的暮年，替他们代言的演说家们说：他们操劳、冒险、牺牲，为的是子子孙孙能够安享太平与智慧，坐食大地的果实。好吧，他们的子孙后代就在这儿——而这里只有骚动。应许之地在哪儿？历次战争里走出来的士兵应许过它；拓荒者把它应许给这一代人；可如今待在这儿的，恰恰就是应许所许给的子子孙孙，轮到他们来操劳、冒险、牺牲——为了什么？

城市粗厉的吼声从敞开的窗口灌进来，一下一下敲打比布斯的耳朵，敲得他渐渐从里面分辨出一记搏动——一段断断续续、不合板眼的抑扬。他觉得那像一条巨人的嗓子，走了调，嘶哑，锈蚀而带金属声——那是神的声音，庞大之神。而这声音召唤比布斯，正像它召唤自己的一切仆人。

===Original===
“Come and work!” it seemed to yell. “Come and work for Me, all men! By your youth and your hope I summon you! By your age and your despair I summon you to work for Me yet a little, with what strength you have. By your love of home I summon you! By your love of woman I summon you! By your hope of children I summon you!

“You shall be blind slaves of Mine, blind to everything but Me, your Master and Driver! For your reward you shall gaze only upon my ugliness. You shall give your toil and your lives, you shall go mad for love and worship of my ugliness! You shall perish still worshipping Me, and your children shall perish knowing no other god!”

And then, as Bibbs closed the window down tight, he heard his father’s voice booming in the next room; he could not distinguish the words but the tone was exultant﻿—and there came the *thump! thump!* of the maimed hand. Bibbs guessed that Sheridan was bragging of the city and of Bigness to some visitor from out-of-town.

===Chinese===
“来做工！”那声音好像在吼叫。“都来给我做工，普天下的人！我凭你们的青春和指望召唤你们！我凭你们的暮年和绝望召唤你们，把你们剩下的那点力气也拿出来，再给我做一阵工。我凭你们对家园的爱召唤你们！我凭你们对女人的爱召唤你们！我凭你们对儿女的指望召唤你们！

“你们要作我瞎眼的奴隶，除了我——你们的主子和赶你们的鞭子——什么也不许看见！算作你们的报偿，你们只许凝望我的丑陋。你们要交出劳苦和性命，你们要爱我的丑陋、拜我的丑陋，爱拜到发疯！你们要灭亡，灭亡的时候还崇拜着我；你们的儿女也要灭亡，此外不识任何别的神！”

随后，比布斯把窗户摇得严严实实，听见隔壁屋里父亲的声音嗡嗡地响；字句听不真切，可那语气是扬扬得意的——还夹着那只残手*砰！砰！*的敲击。比布斯猜得出来，谢里丹正向哪一位外乡来的客人夸说这座城市，夸说庞大。

===Original===
And he thought how truly Sheridan was the high priest of Bigness. But with the old, old thought again, “What for?” Bibbs caught a glimmer of far, faint light. He saw that Sheridan had all his life struggled and conquered, and must all his life go on struggling and inevitably conquering, as part of a vast impulse not his own. Sheridan served blindly﻿—but was the impulse blind? Bibbs asked himself if it was not he who had been in the greater hurry, after all. The kiln must be fired before the vase is glazed, and the Acropolis was not crowned with marble in a day.

Then the voice came to him again, but there was a strain in it as of some high music struggling to be born of the turmoil. “Ugly I am,” it seemed to say to him, “but never forget that I *am* a god!” And the voice grew in sonorousness and in dignity. “The highest should serve, but so long as you worship me for my own sake I will not serve you. It is man who makes me ugly, by his worship of me. If man would let me serve him, I should be beautiful!”

Looking once more from the window, Bibbs sculptured for himself﻿—in the vague contortions of the smoke and fog above the roofs﻿—a gigantic figure with feet pedestaled upon the great buildings and shoulders disappearing in the clouds, a colossus of steel and wholly blackened with soot. But Bibbs carried his fancy further﻿—for there was still a little poet lingering in the back of his head﻿—and he thought that up over the clouds, unseen from below, the giant labored with his hands in the clean sunshine; and Bibbs had a glimpse of what he made there﻿—perhaps for a fellowship of the children of the children that were children now﻿—a noble and joyous city, unbelievably white﻿—

===Chinese===
他想，谢里丹真是庞大之神的大祭司，一点不假。可是那个老而又老的念头又来了——“为了什么？”——这一回，比布斯瞥见了一线遥远而微弱的光。他看出，谢里丹一辈子挣扎、征服，往后还得一辈子挣扎，而且必然照样征服，因为他只是附在一股浩大的冲动上，那股冲动并不是他自己的。谢里丹盲目地侍奉——可那股冲动盲目吗？比布斯问自己：说到底，赶得太急的，莫不是他自己？窑总得先烧，然后才谈得上给瓶上釉；雅典卫城（Acropolis）也不是一天就戴上大理石冠冕的。

接着那声音又来到他这里，可这一回，声音里有一缕调子，仿佛至高的乐音正挣扎着要从骚动中诞生。“我是丑的，”那声音好像对他说，“可永远别忘记：我*是*一位神！”那声音越来越洪亮，越来越庄严。“至高的本该服事人；可只要你们崇拜我是为着我本身的缘故，我就不服事你们。是人把我弄丑的，拿他们对我的崇拜。倘若人肯让我服事他，我就美了！”

比布斯再一次从窗口望出去，在屋顶上方烟雾与雾瘴的模糊翻卷之中，给自己雕出一个巨人的形象：双足立座在大楼之上，双肩隐没进云里，一座钢铁的巨像，通身让煤灰熏得乌黑。可比布斯把他的想象又向前推了一程——因为他脑壳后头还逗留着一个小小的诗人——他想，在云的上头，从底下望不见的地方，那巨人正把双手在洁净的阳光里操劳；比布斯瞥见了他在那里造的东西——也许是造给一个共同体的，那共同体由如今还是孩子的人日后所生的孩子们结成——一座高贵而欢悦的城，白得叫人不敢相信——

---

===Original===
It was the telephone that called him from his vision. It rang fiercely.

He lifted the thing from his desk and answered﻿—and as the small voice inside it spoke he dropped the receiver with a crash. He trembled violently as he picked it up, but he told himself he was wrong﻿—he had been mistaken﻿—yet it was a startlingly beautiful voice; startlingly kind, too, and ineffably like the one he hungered most to hear.

“Who?” he said, his own voice shaking﻿—like his hand.

“Mary.”

===Chinese===
把他从幻象里唤回来的，是电话。电话铃凶猛地响了起来。

他把那东西从书桌上拿起来，应了一声——里面那个小小的声音一开口，他当啷一声，把听筒掉了下去。他抖得厉害地把听筒重新拾起来，却告诉自己他错了——他听岔了——可那确确实实是一个美得惊人的声音；而且和善得也叫人吃惊，还说不出的像——像他最饥渴地想听见的那一个声音。

“哪一位？”他说，他自己的声音在抖——像他的手一样。

“玛丽。”

===Original===
He responded with two hushed and incredulous words: “*Is it?*”

There was a little thrill of pathetic half-laughter in the instrument. “Bibbs﻿—I wanted to﻿—just to see if you﻿—”

“Yes﻿—Mary?”

===Chinese===
他压着嗓子答了两个字，满是不敢相信：“*是你么？*”

听筒里传来一阵微微的颤音，是叫人心酸的、半截子的笑。“比布斯——我想——只想问问你是不是——”

“是——玛丽？”

===Original===
“I was looking when you were so nearly run over. I saw it, Bibbs. They said you hadn’t been hurt, they thought, but I wanted to know for myself.”

“No, no, I wasn’t hurt at all﻿—Mary. It was father who came nearer it. He saved me.”

“Yes, I saw; but you had fallen. I couldn’t get through the crowd until you had gone. And I wanted to *know*.”

“Mary﻿—would you﻿—have minded?” he said.

===Chinese===
“你差一点儿叫车撞着的那个当口，我正在看着。我看见了，比布斯。他们说没伤着你，他们是这么估摸的，可我要自己知道。”

“没有，没有，我一点儿也没伤着——玛丽。挨得更险的是父亲。是他救了我。”

“是，我看见了；可你摔倒了。人堆里我怎么也挤不过来，一直等到你们走了才成。我非要*知道*不可。”

“玛丽——若是那样——你会在意么？”他说。

===Original===
There was a long interval before she answered.

“Yes.”

“Then why﻿—”

“Yes, Bibbs?”

===Chinese===
她隔了好长一会儿才答话。

“会在意。”

“那为什么——”

“嗯，比布斯？”

===Original===
“I don’t know what to say,” he cried. “It’s so wonderful to hear your voice again﻿—I’m shaking, Mary﻿—I﻿—I don’t know﻿—I don’t know anything except that I *am* talking to you! It *is* you﻿—Mary?”

“Yes, Bibbs!”

“Mary﻿—I’ve seen you from my window at home﻿—only five times since I﻿—since then. You looked﻿—oh, how can I tell you? It was like a man chained in a cave catching a glimpse of the blue sky, Mary. Mary, won’t you﻿—let me see you again﻿—near? I think I could make you really forgive me﻿—you’d have to﻿—”

“I *did*﻿—then.”

===Chinese===
“我不知道说什么好，”他喊道。“能再听见你的声音，太不可思议了——我在抖，玛丽——我——我不知道——除了我*确实*在同你说话，我什么都不知道！*真是*你——玛丽？”

“是，比布斯！”

“玛丽——在家的时候，我从窗口望见过你——自打我——自打那回起，统共只望见过五回。你那样子——哦，叫我怎么告诉你呢？就像一个锁在洞窟里的人瞥见了一眼蓝天，玛丽。玛丽，你肯不肯——让我再见你一回——近近地见？我想我能叫你真真正正地饶恕我——你非饶恕不可——”

“我*确实*饶恕过——那时候。”

===Original===
“No﻿—not really﻿—or you wouldn’t have said you couldn’t see me any more.”

“That wasn’t the reason.” The voice was very low.

“Mary,” he said, even more tremulously than before, “I can’t﻿—you *couldn’t* mean it was because﻿—you can’t mean it was because you﻿—care?”

There was no answer.

===Chinese===
“不——不是真的——不然你就不会说，你再不能见我了。”

“那不是缘故。”那声音非常低。

“玛丽，”他说，比先前抖得更凶了，“我不能——你*不会*是说，那是因为——你不能是想说，那是因为你——把我放在心上？”

没有回答。

===Original===
“Mary?” he called, huskily. “If you mean *that*﻿—you’d let me see you﻿—wouldn’t you?”

And now the voice was so low he could not be sure it spoke at all, but if it did, the words were, “Yes, Bibbs﻿—dear.”

But the voice was not in the instrument﻿—it was so gentle and so light, so almost nothing, it seemed to be made of air﻿—and it came from the air.

Slowly and incredulously he turned﻿—and glory fell upon his shining eyes. The door of his father’s room had opened.

Mary stood upon the threshold.

===Chinese===
“玛丽？”他哑着嗓子唤。“你要是*这个*意思——你就会让我见你——是不是？”

这时候，那声音低极了，他简直拿不准它究竟出没出声；可倘若出了声，那话便是：“是的，比布斯——亲爱的。”

可那声音并不在听筒里——它那么温柔、那么轻，轻得几乎没有，仿佛是空气做成——而它是从空气里来的。

他慢慢地、不敢相信地转过身去——荣光落在他那双发亮的眼睛上。父亲那间屋子的门开了。

玛丽站在门槛上。
